#602. APOD: 2026 November 16 – NGC 7129: Star Formation with Jets from Webb

Discover the cosmos! Each day a different image or photograph of our fascinating universe is featured, along with a brief explanation written by a professional astronomer.

Explanation: How do stars form? To help answer this question, NASA used the James Webb Space Telescope to take the featured detailed image of the star-forming region NGC 7129 in infrared light. Star formation progresses when gravity pulls together massive amounts of gas, but soon things can get complicated when previously formed stars emit powerful jets and winds -- and some then explode. Opaque dust can form in the cool atmospheres of stars and, when dispersed, can obstruct visible-light images. Many stars can be seen to be forming in the featured image, with the young stars on the left, in the gold-colored gas, typically being more mature than the young stars on the right -- in the red region. Jets and winds likely cause the filamentary nature of the red-colored gas. Studying stars at different stages of development bolsters understanding about what physical processes are most important and when.Tomorrow's picture: sky duel

NASA explores the unknown in air and space, innovates for the benefit of humanity, and inspires the world through discovery.

#601. Moon phase today: What the Moon will look like on November 15, 2026

There's a little more to see on the Moon tonight as the crescent continues to fill out, so if you're into Moon gazing, grab your viewing gear and head outside.

As of Sunday, Nov. 15, the NASA's Daily Moon Guide tracker tells us the Moon is in the Waxing Crescent phase, with 30% of its surface visible.

If you don't have any visual aids, you can still see the Mare Crisium and the Mare Fecunditatis. With binoculars or a telescope, you'll also see the Endymion Crater, Posidonius Crater, and the Mare Nectaris. And for keen Moon gazers with a telescope, you'll get to see all this plus the Apollo 11 and 17 landing spots.

The next Full Moon will take place on November 24.

The Moon takes approximately 29.5 days to complete one full cycle. As it orbits Earth, the amount of sunlight reflecting off its surface that we can see changes, creating the different phases. This takes us from the New Moon to the Full Moon and back again, before the cycle begins once more, according to NASA.

New Moon - The Moon is between Earth and the sun, so the side we see is dark (in other words, it's invisible to the eye).

Waxing Crescent - A small sliver of light appears on the right side (Northern Hemisphere).

First Quarter - Half of the Moon is lit on the right side. It looks like a half-Moon.

Waxing Gibbous - More than half is lit up, but it's not quite full yet.

Full Moon - The whole face of the Moon is illuminated and fully visible.

Waning Gibbous - The Moon starts losing light on the right side. (Northern Hemisphere)

Third Quarter (or Last Quarter) - Another half-Moon, but now the left side is lit.

Waning Crescent - A thin sliver of light remains on the left side before going dark again.

You can find bylines in publications including Fit&Well, Metro, and Coach magazine, usually covering deals on everything from earbuds to TVs, or guides on how to beat your half marathon time.

#600. Alzheimer’s may start shrinking two brain regions years before memory loss

Alzheimer's disease may begin leaving detectable clues in the brain as much as a decade before memory problems appear. Researchers at Johns Hopkins University have identified two brain regions that show signs of deterioration earlier than the hippocampus, the area commonly examined for evidence of the disease.

The discovery could eventually help doctors recognize Alzheimer's years before symptoms develop, potentially allowing treatment to begin when it has the greatest chance of protecting the brain.

By examining hundreds of brain scans, the scientists found that the entorhinal cortex and amygdala, two regions involved in memory and emotion, begin losing tissue earlier than the hippocampus. These changes may offer important new clues about how Alzheimer's develops and how it could be detected before significant cognitive decline occurs.

"We have found that there are early changes in the brain manifesting the clinical biomarkers of atrophy as much as a decade before there are clinical symptoms," said biomedical engineer and senior author Michael Miller. "It's at least a 5-to-10-year play where your brain is changing. Imagine if we could start to treat the neurodegenerative changes at the 2, 3 or 4-year point rather than after 15 years when the changes are manifesting in memory symptoms. That's the great promise here for your loved ones who are at risk."

The findings were recently published in NeuroImage Reports and are being presented at Neuroscience 2026, the annual meeting of the Society for Neuroscience.

Detecting Alzheimer's Before Memory Loss Begins

Alzheimer's is the most common cause of dementia worldwide, but the disease does not begin when someone first experiences forgetfulness or confusion. Changes associated with Alzheimer's can accumulate in the brain for decades before noticeable symptoms emerge.

Scientists believe this long period of silent progression may provide a crucial opportunity for treatment. Interventions that begin before extensive damage occurs could potentially do more to preserve memory and other cognitive abilities. However, taking advantage of that opportunity requires reliable ways to identify the disease in its earliest stages.

One promising approach involves biomarkers, measurable biological changes that can indicate the presence or progression of a disease. In Alzheimer's research, these markers could help reveal who is developing the condition before symptoms become apparent.

"Our goal is to be able to treat individuals before the symptoms of Alzheimer's disease develop so that they may delayed or even stopped," said Marilyn Albert, co-author and director of the Johns Hopkins Alzheimer's Disease Research Center.

Two Brain Regions Reveal Earlier Warning Signs

To investigate where the earliest detectable changes occur, the researchers examined hundreds of magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) and positron emission tomography (PET) scans collected through two independent research projects: the Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative and the Biomarkers of Cognitive Decline Among Normal Individuals study.

The scans followed people at different stages of Alzheimer's disease over time, including individuals without symptoms, those experiencing cognitive impairment, and patients who ultimately died from the disease.

In both groups, the researchers observed a consistent pattern. The entorhinal cortex and amygdala, which are located near the hippocampus, showed greater relative tissue loss during the early stages of Alzheimer's than the hippocampus itself.

This distinction matters because the hippocampus plays a central role in memory and has traditionally been a major focus when doctors look for Alzheimer's-related brain damage.

The two newly highlighted regions perform important functions of their own. The entorhinal cortex helps retrieve previously stored memories, while the amygdala contributes to emotional memory, including how emotional experiences are processed and remembered.

Their earlier shrinkage suggests that examining these structures together could reveal signs of Alzheimer's that might be less apparent when attention is focused primarily on the hippocampus.

Brain Shrinkage Linked to Alzheimer's Tau Protein

The researchers also found an important connection between the loss of brain tissue and tau, a protein closely associated with Alzheimer's disease.

Under normal conditions, tau helps maintain the internal structure of nerve cells. In Alzheimer's, however, tau can accumulate abnormally and form tangles that interfere with healthy brain function.

Using PET imaging and digital pathology, the scientists found that areas of gray matter shrinkage overlapped with regions showing tau-related changes. Gray matter contains large numbers of nerve cell bodies and is essential for processing information throughout the brain.

This relationship strengthens the possibility that changes in the entorhinal cortex and amygdala could serve as useful indicators of early Alzheimer's disease.

The team believes that measuring tissue loss in both regions, rather than relying primarily on changes in the hippocampus, could eventually improve predictions of who is likely to develop cognitive decline.

As brain imaging becomes more accessible and less expensive, this approach could also become increasingly practical. For people with a family history of Alzheimer's or other risk factors, researchers envision a future in which doctors might examine these regions as early as a person's 30s or 40s.

"As biomarkers and treatments become increasingly available, knowing who is going to have cognitive decline is going to be really important," said co-author Meaghan Morris, a neuropathologist who studies Alzheimer's and other dementias. "This could really play into better risk stratification for identifying who is going to have cognitive decline in the near future -- and treating people earlier and earlier to hopefully slow the course of the disease."

A Potential New Path Toward Earlier Alzheimer's Treatment

Although the findings are promising, more research is necessary before doctors can use these brain changes as a reliable diagnostic tool. The study identifies potential early biomarkers, but it does not yet establish a clinical test capable of predicting Alzheimer's years in advance.

The next step is to investigate what happens within these regions at a much smaller scale. The researchers want to understand the molecular changes surrounding abnormal tau accumulation and how those changes contribute to the progressive loss of brain tissue.

Identifying the biological processes involved could eventually reveal new targets for therapies designed to interrupt Alzheimer's before extensive damage occurs.

"Our future work is to better show the molecular neighborhoods associated with these Tau protein changes as localized by these clinical biomarkers of atrophy," Miller said. "Once we know more about the molecular neighborhoods, we can develop therapies."

Materials provided by Johns Hopkins University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#598. Microsoft 365 Crawls Back to Life After a 24-Hour Outage

Sorry, Margaret, your email is working again.

After a widespread outage shut down Microsoft 365 services for businesses, governments, and tens of millions of other users around the world, the essential cloud services are now (mostly) back online, according to the company’s status page.

The outage debacle began around lunchtime on the East Coast on Thursday, with users reporting Outlook outages to the service-tracking website Downdetector just after 12 pm ET. Within the hour, Microsoft said it was investigating reports of an outage that at the time was limited to North America. It quickly spread to impact more than 70 countries around the world.

The full cause of Microsoft’s unprecedented outage remains unknown, but the company repeatedly ruled out a cyberattack as the cause in statements to its status page. It is also ruling out AI or AI agents playing a role in the debacle.

“We are seeing broad recovery and stability across our services,” a Microsoft spokesperson says in a statement. “This was a technical issue not related to cyber or AI/agents. The incident was introduced by an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade that surfaced following a recent network change. We have addressed the underlying issue and continue to restore service to remaining customers and regions.”

The company has promised a “full post-incident report” after it has fully reviewed what went wrong.

As Outlook, Teams, Copilot, and other Microsoft 365 systems come back online, users will likely see their inboxes turn into a mess. “You're probably going to get a lot of notifications when things come back,” says Jonathan Leitschuh, an independent researcher.

Microsoft said it had increased the queue time for emails to 48 hours in an attempt to ensure that every message that people tried to send while its systems were down gets delivered. Leitschuh points out that, even with the bulk of systems restored, users may receive “a flood of notifications a few hours down the pike” as subsystems come back online.

The total impact of the Microsoft outage may never be fully known, but it’s safe to say it’s already significant. Governments from Japan to the United States reported disruptions to a bevy of functions. Airlines reported problems with their ability to communicate with customers. Many law enforcement and emergency services ground to a halt. And hospitals reported widespread interruptions to their operations.

Ultimately, the episode serves as a reminder of the risks involved when the whole world relies on a single company for a critical service. “We're very interdependent on these systems in modern life,” says Leitschuh. Next time, delayed emails may be the least of our worries.

#597. Hurdle hints and answers for November 15, 2026

If you like playing daily word games like Wordle, then Hurdle is a great game to add to your routine.

There are five rounds to the game. The first round sees you trying to guess the word, with correct, misplaced, and incorrect letters shown in each guess. If you guess the correct answer, it'll take you to the next hurdle, providing the answer to the last hurdle as your first guess. This can give you several clues or none, depending on the words. For the final hurdle, every correct answer from previous hurdles is shown, with correct and misplaced letters clearly shown.

An important note is that the number of times a letter is highlighted from previous guesses does necessarily indicate the number of times that letter appears in the final hurdle.

#596. Small retailers brace for 'huge hit' this holiday season as shipping costs spike

When Jenni Shields began her small business eight years ago selling handmade hair accessories, she paid $2.93 to ship an order, or close to $4 in today's dollars when adjusted for inflation.

Last year, Shields said, an average shipping label for her products cost around $4.50. But when she took a typical batch of orders to her local U.S. Postal Service office last month in Kansas City, Kansas, she was shocked to see that it was an average of nearly $6 per package.

Shields is just one of the small- and medium-sized retailers who are struggling to keep up with rising shipping costs as the price of fuel continues to trend upward.

Small business owners such as Shields are facing a difficult holiday season. Customer demand is strong, but some retailers said their profits will be dented — if not eaten up entirely — by higher shipping and other costs.

Shields said she charges her customers $6 in shipping fees, which includes roughly $1.50 to cover her packaging supplies. The dramatic upswing this year means she's no longer breaking even on her orders and instead eating the added costs, she said.

"I am losing money at this point, and I've really taken a huge hit this year," said Shields, the owner of Evie Pearl Handmade, which sells bows, quilted headbands and other accessories. "I feel like it's just been this slow increase up [in shipping costs], and I feel horrible."

The surge in fuel prices since the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran in February has driven up the cost of shipping by sea, air, rail and road.

That means higher costs across the board, including for shipping giant Amazon, which reported an 11% increase in shipping expenses in the third quarter compared with last year, to individual retailers.

Globally, air cargo is going for about $3.37 per kilogram, up 43% from a similar period a year ago, according to transportation marketplace Freightos. Judah Levine, Freightos' head of research, said in addition to higher fuel, demand for fast shipping of hardware used to build data centers is helping to boost air freight rates, even weeks before peak last-minute holiday packages take to the skies.

For businesses, that runup is going to "squeeze their margins in the near-term," said Jason Seidl, managing director and transportation analyst at TD Cowen. "Larger companies tend not to be exposed to the spot market, and they'll have what we call dedicated deals in place."

But that doesn't help smaller companies, which are walking a delicate line of not wanting to alienate customers who have enjoyed free shipping for nearly a generation.

Todd Shelton, founder of the eponymous 23-year-old New Jersey clothing company that makes high-end men's jeans, T-shirts and button-up shirts locally, said he started charging customers for return shipping this week for the first time after absorbing the higher expenses this year. The shipping is deducted from customers' refunds.

Shelton said his costs have been rising for materials, too, such as his imported Japanese denim and shirt fabric from Portugal. The company is still profitable, but the higher costs have eaten into profit margins as they stay elevated, he's considering raising prices or charging for shipping to customers, he said.

Shields said she's considering raising her shipping fees for the first time in six years to keep up with the increases.

The holiday season is one of Shields' busiest. But she said she's seen a slowdown in sales because of shipping fees.

"I can't compete with Amazon on the free shipping. I can't do that. I can't afford that," she said. "I'm seeing so much of my percentage that's supposed to come back to me and my family being put towards the shipping costs."

With no sign of fuel prices declining significantly, Shields, who runs her business out of her spare bedroom at home, said she's not sure that small businesses will be able to emerge on the other side of the holidays.

Shippers including UPS, FedEx and USPS have all instituted holiday surcharges, with prices varying by package type and lasting through the end of the season. Though that's typical during the peak holiday period each year, those charges have continued to rise over the past few years.

But even as costs everywhere climb, consumers are still spending.

According to the National Retail Federation, retail sales through the end of the year are expected to grow 4.2%, translating to total spending of $1.01 trillion. That's compared with last year's holiday sales totaling $972 billion, which was 3.1% growth.

Some of that growth is from inflation, meaning consumers are simply paying more for the same goods.

"Customers all expect to spend more this holiday, but part of why they expect to spend more is they expect things to cost more, not because they'd like to spend more or they were hoping to get more quantity of stuff," said Aaron Cheris, the global retail practice head at Bain & Company. "So what I suspect happens is that non-discretionary categories do fine, because you buy the food you were going to need to buy anyway, but then the amount of cash that's left over for [discretionary] goods is less."

Still, Cheris said it's shaping up to be a record year for shipping costs, which is putting immense pressure on smaller retailers. That means many retailers may find themselves in financial distress in the first quarter of next year as they struggle to make it through the holiday season.

He added that larger retail companies will also have to reckon with rising costs, though they may not feel those effects until later quarters because they have shipping contracts that have already been locked in and have control over their own delivery fleets.

"If anything, it benefits the scale players over the little players, but it's kind of a little bit bad for everybody," Cheris said. "I think that is the challenge."

#595. Hidden ecosystem: Scientists discover 245 animals living inside a single deep-sea rock

Deep beneath the Pacific Ocean, scientists have uncovered a surprising world of tiny animals living inside metal-rich rocks that take millions of years to form. These rocks, known as polymetallic nodules, contain miniature cracks and cavities that provide shelter for creatures smaller than a millimeter.

Although they may look like ordinary stones, these nodules offer something unusual in the deep ocean. They create hard surfaces in an environment dominated by soft mud, while their internal spaces provide additional habitats for marine life.

Polymetallic nodules are dark, roughly fist-sized formations made largely of iron and manganese oxides. They grow at an extraordinarily slow pace, often adding only a few millimeters over a million years. Found at ocean depths ranging from 4,000 to 6,000 meters, they can remain on the seafloor for millions of years.

Researchers set out to investigate the tiny organisms living within these ancient formations and determine how important the nodules are to deep-sea biodiversity.

Dr. Tanja Stratmann, a deep-sea ecologist and one of the study's three lead authors, explains: "In our study we show that the sizes of cracks inside the nodules are large enough to provide home to animals that are smaller than a millimeter in size."

Hundreds of Tiny Animals Found Inside a Single Rock

To explore these hidden habitats, the research team collected nodules from the Clarion-Clipperton Zone, a vast region of the central Pacific Ocean. They used specialized sampling equipment, including box corers that collect sections of the seafloor and remotely operated diving robots capable of working at extreme depths.

Back in the laboratory, the researchers carefully opened the nodules and extracted the tiny animals concealed within their cracks and crevices.

The results revealed an unexpectedly rich community of marine life. Across 244 nodules, the team identified 4,139 individual animals. Each nodule contained an average of 17 animals, but one particularly crowded specimen sheltered an astonishing 245 individuals.

The discovery shows that these rocks are more than simple surfaces where marine organisms can attach themselves. Their intricate internal structures create three-dimensional living spaces, allowing animals to inhabit protected areas within the nodules themselves.

Dr. Coral Diaz-Recio Lorenzo explains: "The crevices in the polymetallic nodules function as small shelters and provide home to animals that we do not encounter in the soft sediment of the seafloor."

Hidden Ecosystems Unlike Those on the Surrounding Seafloor

Although the nodules contained fewer animals than the surrounding muddy sediment, their contribution to deep-sea life was still substantial.

For roundworms (nematodes), the number living inside the nodules amounted to approximately 10% of the number found in the surrounding sediment. For tiny crustaceans (copepods), the figure was about 25%.

However, the most important finding was not simply how many animals the researchers discovered. It was how different the communities inside the rocks were from those living in the nearby mud.

Genetic analyses revealed that the animals inhabiting the nodules formed distinctive communities. Remarkably, these communities remained highly similar across locations separated by large distances (up to 1,000 km), even though they differed significantly from the organisms found in the surrounding seafloor sediment.

This suggests that polymetallic nodules support specialized habitats that are not easily replaced by other parts of the deep ocean. Their cracks and cavities offer living conditions that certain organisms appear to depend on.

Deep-Sea Mining Could Destroy These Ancient Habitats

The findings raise serious concerns about the potential environmental consequences of deep-sea mining.

Polymetallic nodules contain economically valuable metals, including nickel, cobalt, and copper. These resources have attracted interest in industrial mining operations that would collect the nodules directly from the ocean floor.

However, removing the rocks would also eliminate the miniature habitats they contain, along with the animals living inside them.

One example is Thurammina sp. 1, a single-celled organism found particularly frequently within the nodules. This species appears especially well adapted to the sheltered spaces inside the rocks, making it an example of the specialized life that could be threatened by their removal.

The problem is that these habitats cannot quickly recover. Because polymetallic nodules require millions of years to develop, new ones would not form within a human lifetime. Mining could therefore eliminate ecosystems that would take geological timescales to replace.

Prof. Sabine Gollner warns: "Our findings point out that commercial mining may result in an irreversible loss of undescribed and unique biodiversity associated to nodules. This needs to be considered in environmental management of deep-sea mining."

Scientists Race to Understand What Could Be Lost

The discovery highlights an often overlooked aspect of ocean conservation. Protecting deep-sea ecosystems requires more than safeguarding large, visible animals. It also means considering the microscopic and nearly microscopic organisms that inhabit specialized environments hidden within the seafloor.

An important question remains unanswered: If deep-sea mining destroys these nodule communities, could some species disappear entirely?

Researchers still need to determine whether organisms that depend on the nodules also inhabit other parts of the ocean. If certain species are restricted to these habitats, removing the nodules could potentially contribute to their global extinction.

Understanding where these animals live and how widely they are distributed will be essential for identifying areas that need protection and evaluating the potential ecological damage caused by deep-sea mining.

To investigate further, Dr. Tanja Stratmann is currently participating in an international research expedition to the Clarion-Clipperton Zone, where scientists are continuing to study the ecosystems associated with polymetallic nodules.

This expedition and other follow-up investigations are independently funded through the JPIOcean projects MiningImpact 2 and 3.

The findings reveal that even a single rock on the deep ocean floor can support a surprisingly complex community of life. As interest in harvesting the ocean's mineral resources grows, scientists are working to understand these hidden ecosystems before habitats that took millions of years to form are permanently lost.

Materials provided by MARUM - Center for Marine Environmental Sciences, University of Bremen. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#590. Astronomers discover a dead star spinning every 8 minutes and sending mysterious radio pulses

Astronomers have traced mysterious radio signals to an unusual pair of stars more than 5,000 light-years from Earth. One is a dead star that rotates every eight minutes, while its companion closely resembles our own Sun.

The discovery was made by researchers at the Curtin University branch of the International Centre for Radio Astronomy Research (ICRAR). Their findings reveal a previously unseen combination of stars producing radio pulses and offer new clues about the origins of some of the Milky Way's most puzzling signals.

The stellar system, designated GPM J071507−114153, contains a white dwarf and a G-type star orbiting one another. A white dwarf is the extremely dense remnant of a star that has exhausted its nuclear fuel and shed its outer layers. Its companion belongs to the same general category of stars as the Sun.

Until now, astronomers had detected similar radio pulses in systems containing white dwarfs and smaller, cooler stars known as red dwarfs. This marks the first time such signals have been identified in a system where a white dwarf orbits a Sun-like star.

A Rare Star System With Mysterious Radio Pulses

The newly identified pair belongs to an unusual group of astronomical objects called long-period transients (LPTs). These objects produce radio emissions that appear and disappear over periods lasting anywhere from several minutes to hours.

Despite growing interest in these strange signals, their origins remain poorly understood. The same ICRAR research team discovered the first known example in 2021, and only about a dozen have been identified since then.

Some of these systems contain a white dwarf alongside another star, but astronomers have struggled to determine which member of the pair is responsible for the radio emissions.

The latest discovery provides an important breakthrough. For the first time, researchers have obtained radio observations detailed enough to follow a white dwarf as it travels around its stellar companion. This allows them to pinpoint the likely location of the radio pulses much more accurately.

"This is the first time the pulses are clear enough to see the white dwarf moving in its orbit around its companion, so we know that the pulsations are coming from a region close to the white dwarf, or possibly even from the white dwarf itself," said lead author Dr. Sam McSweeney.

McSweeney, now a commissioning scientist with the Square Kilometre Array Observatory, made the discovery while working at Curtin University's ICRAR branch.

To confirm the findings and examine the white dwarf's orbital motion, the researchers analyzed more than 100 hours of radio observations using computing resources at the Pawsey Supercomputing Research Centre.

Giant Magnetic Storms Detected 5,000 Light-Years Away

The radio signals revealed more than the movement of the dead star. They also gave astronomers a surprising opportunity to investigate magnetic activity on its Sun-like companion.

According to study co-author Associate Professor Natasha Hurley-Walker, the observations exposed stellar flares associated with powerful magnetic disturbances. Similar magnetic processes on our own Sun can lead to auroras on Earth when energetic particles interact with our planet's magnetic field and atmosphere.

The team also detected evidence that the white dwarf passed behind an especially magnetically active region near its companion.

"As a bonus, we also saw the white dwarf moving behind a region with a strong magnetic field, most likely a 'stellar prominence', which is a large loop of plasma erupting from a star's surface."

A stellar prominence consists of electrically charged gas held in enormous loops by magnetic fields. These structures can extend far above a star's surface, providing clues about the powerful forces shaping its outer atmosphere.

The ability to detect such activity across thousands of light-years demonstrates how much information astronomers can extract from radio signals, even when the stars themselves are extraordinarily distant.

"It's amazing that by using radio wavelengths we can detect the storms on a star over 5000 light-years away."

Powerful Radio Telescopes Reveal a Hidden Stellar Pair

ICRAR researchers have played a leading role in identifying long-period transients, and the latest discovery relied on observations from several major astronomical facilities around the world.

The system was initially detected during a sweeping radio survey of the Milky Way conducted with the MWA (Murchison Widefield Array) in Western Australia.

"We detected this system in a large survey using the MWA (Murchison Widefield Array) to scan the entire Milky Way visible from Australia. We then used the MeerKAT radio telescope in South Africa to determine its precise position," said Associate Professor Hurley-Walker.

After the radio telescopes narrowed down the system's location, astronomers turned to the W.M. Keck Observatory on Maunakea, Hawaiʻi Island. Optical observations confirmed that the radio source was associated with a G-type star, establishing the presence of a companion similar to the Sun.

The MWA, led by Curtin University, operates at Inyarrimanha Ilgari Bundara, the CSIRO Murchison Radio-Astronomy Observatory on Wajarri Yamaji Country in Western Australia.

The MeerKAT telescope was built and is operated by the South African Radio Astronomy Observatory in South Africa's North Cape province.

Both instruments are precursors to the next generation of Square Kilometre Array telescopes. The Australian facility, SKA-Low, and the South African facility, SKA-Mid, are being developed to provide much greater sensitivity and detail when observing the radio universe.

For astronomers studying rare stellar systems, these future capabilities could dramatically expand the number of objects they can discover and reveal much more about the processes responsible for their unusual signals.

Dr. McSweeney said, "If we are already making such discoveries with precursor telescopes, just imagine how many more star systems we will be able to find, and what a beautifully detailed view of their signals we will have, once the SKAO telescopes are completely built."

The findings were published in Nature Astronomy in a study titled "A long-period transient with a Sun-like companion."

Materials provided by International Centre for Radio Astronomy Research. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#585. Where's the growth? Legacy automakers face a tougher 2027 as costs rise, market tightens

DETROIT — There's a growing concern that the global automotive industry may be stuck in neutral, if not reverse, heading into 2027.

The auto industry — from the companies to their customers — has been surprisingly resilient this decade, but there are fears that rising costs, weaker vehicle pricing and a more challenging market could threaten profits and growth going forward.

Automakers also face pressure outside the U.S. from an onslaught of Chinese carmakers, a thinner new-product pipeline after retreating from some all-electric plans, and company- and region-specific challenges.

"With investor focus increasingly shifting to 2027, the setup appears challenging," Barclays analyst Dan Levy said in an investor note last month. "Rising inflation, continued China pressure, affordability concerns in the U.S., and a flat global production outlook suggest another year of limited earnings growth."

Several Wall Street analysts have said automakers will need continued pricing strength and additional cost savings to offset inflation, higher investment spending and a potentially weaker economy just to maintain their current levels.

"It's a little bit more optimistic in the U.S. and then very pessimistic in Europe," RBC Capital Markets analyst Tom Narayan told CNBC. "As a whole, there's some negativity on '27."

Automakers from the U.S. to Europe to Asia have been facing a growing list of headwinds.

For U.S.-based legacy automakers, Ford Motor said it expects next year's results to be in line with 2026, while General Motors grew more cautious about its ability to continue growing earnings as much as it had previously expected.

"We continue to believe that 2027 will be another strong year and better than 2026, assuming customer demand remains strong, and commodity, logistics and other recent cost pressures moderate," GM CEO Mary Barra told investors Oct. 20.

GM CFO Paul Jacobson said that while it's too early to officially forecast, the company is facing additional inflationary costs that could lead to another $2 billion headwind in 2027, like it expects this year. He specifically cited costs of commodity inflation, logistics and DRAM, or dynamic random-access memory chips as challenges.

Those chips are critical components in modern vehicles and are used for everything from temporary data storage and real-time processing from infotainment systems to safety-critical systems.

GM and others have been able to offset outside pressures through cost cuts, operational efficiencies and resilient consumer demand for higher-margin vehicles. However, there's a limit to what companies can do.

"There's only so much pricing you can push to consumers," Narayan said. "They're already at pretty high pricing."

European automakers including Volkswagen, Mercedes-Benz and BMW in recent weeks reduced their 2026 earnings forecasts in response to U.S. tariffs, the growth of Chinese automakers and rising costs.

Most recently, BMW this week said accelerated restructuring measures would reduce its automotive pretax earnings margin this year by 2.5 percentage points. The company said the restructuring should support "positive effects" into 2027 and a 3% to 5% pretax profit margin for 2028. That compares with a 5.3% automotive margin last year and 3.4% during the third quarter.

Chrysler parent Stellantis, which has large operations in Europe and the U.S., is facing its own challenges, as it's in the midst of a restructuring that aims for a low-single-digit adjusted operating margin this year. It has maintained a 2026 guidance that includes a mid-single-digit percentage increase in net revenue and a low-single-digit adjusted operating margin.

In Asia, South Korean automaker Hyundai Motor, which has experienced notable growth in recent years, also cut key metrics when it reported earnings late last month. The company lowered its 2026 wholesales target to 4 million vehicles, down from 4.16 million, and cut its operating-margin target to between 5.5% and 6.5%, down from a previous goal of 6.3% to 7.3%.

Toyota Motor is an outlier for its most recent quarter and outlook, as the world's largest automaker increased its full-year operating income forecast by 500 billion Japanese yen (about $3.2 billion) on Thursday.

Toyota's results were boosted by weakness in the Japanese yen as well as strong hybrid vehicle demand in the U.S. However, the increase in operating income was its first in six quarters.

Broadly, the changes to earnings and outlooks come as production for the global industry slows.

Consulting and insights firm GlobalData expects global light-vehicle production of about 90.96 million units in 2026, down 2.1% compared with last year. It forecast a production increase of less than 1% next year. That comes on the heels of strong annual growth earlier this decade, led by China.

Cox Automotive reported the average transaction price of a new U.S. vehicle in September was more than $50,500. That was just shy of the record of $50,614 in December 2025.

Lofty vehicle prices have led to record-high and record-long financing deals, as well as concerns that customers are getting priced out of the new-vehicle market.

The typical U.S. new-vehicle payment reached $776 a month in September, the highest since December 2023, according to Cox Automotive.

Cox Automotive chief economist Jeremy Robb, discussing the third quarter for the automotive industry, rated the overall economy, buyer ability and borrowing costs as cautionary "yellow lights."

But he did have some "green lights," such as good credit availability and employment conditions, which could give some relief for automakers and consumers, many of whom need vehicles for their everyday lives.

Robb said there's a "tug-of-war" between headwinds such as rising costs, interest rates and fuel prices and tailwinds such as growth in liquid assets and savings that has helped offset some of the additional costs.

"Consumers remain under financial pressure, but the vehicle demand continues to hold, showing more resiliency in the auto industry than we've seen in other parts of the economy," Robb said during a Sept. 24 call, noting consumer sentiment remains low.

#584. Uranus and Neptune’s bizarre magnetic fields may finally be explained

When NASA's Voyager 2 spacecraft visited Uranus and Neptune in the 1980s, it discovered something extraordinary. Unlike Earth, which has a relatively orderly magnetic field with clearly defined north and south poles, both distant planets possess strangely distorted and dramatically tilted magnetic fields. For decades, scientists have struggled to explain why.

Now, researchers believe they may have found an answer hidden deep inside these enormous worlds. A new study suggests that changes in how well materials conduct electricity under extreme conditions could explain the planets' unusual magnetism. The findings also raise an intriguing possibility: the magnetic fields of Uranus and Neptune may change dramatically over just a few decades.

The research, published in Communications Physics, was led by scientists at The University of Texas at Austin in collaboration with researchers from the University of California, Los Angeles, Imperial College London, and Northumbria University.

Why Uranus and Neptune Have Such Strange Magnetic Fields

Planetary magnetic fields originate deep beneath the surface, where moving fluids capable of conducting electricity generate magnetic forces. Scientists call this process a dynamo.

On Earth, the dynamo operates within the outer core, a layer of molten metal surrounded by mostly solid rock. As liquid iron circulates and the planet rotates, it generates a magnetic field resembling that of a giant bar magnet. This produces the familiar north and south magnetic poles that allow compasses to point in a consistent direction.

Earth's magnetic field is also closely aligned with its rotation axis. Its main magnetic axis, known as a dipole, is tilted by only 11 degrees. Uranus and Neptune are strikingly different. Uranus has a magnetic dipole tilted by 59 degrees, while Neptune's is tilted by 47 degrees.

Explaining these extreme tilts requires a better understanding of what lies inside the two planets.

Uranus and Neptune are classified as ice giants, a name that reflects their presumed composition rather than suggesting they are simply frozen worlds. Each is approximately four times wider than Earth, and scientists believe their interiors contain substantial amounts of hydrogen, helium, water, ammonia, methane, and rocky material. However, their precise internal compositions remain uncertain.

Extreme Conditions Deep Inside the Ice Giants

Chi Yan, the study's lead author and a research associate at the UT Jackson School of Geosciences, believes the combination of the planets' enormous size and unusual chemical makeup may help explain their distorted magnetic fields.

"Deep inside Uranus and Neptune, pressures and temperatures become extremely high," said Yan. "Under these conditions, which can cause water molecules to break apart into ions, the material may become increasingly electrically conductive with depth."

Ions are electrically charged particles that can carry electric current. If the materials inside Uranus and Neptune become more conductive at greater depths, that could significantly influence how their magnetic fields are generated.

To investigate this possibility, the researchers developed computer simulations designed to reproduce the strange magnetic fields recorded by Voyager 2.

Their models assumed that electrical conductivity increases deeper inside the planets. The researchers also tested a variety of possible internal structures, examining how circulating fluids, temperature changes, and magnetic forces interact over time.

The results suggest that these changing electrical properties could play a crucial role in producing the unusual magnetic fields observed around both ice giants.

Magnetic Poles Could Shift Dramatically Within Decades

Perhaps the most surprising finding is that the magnetic fields of Uranus and Neptune may be far less stable than Earth's.

According to the simulations, their magnetic axes could undergo substantial changes in relatively short periods. In some scenarios, the magnetic tilt shifted by tens of degrees within only a few decades.

That raises the possibility that the magnetic fields surrounding Uranus and Neptune today are significantly different from those measured when Voyager 2 flew past them in the 1980s.

"Earth's magnetic tilt drifts by only a degree or so over 50 years," said Yan. "Our models show the ice giants can behave very differently. In some stretches the tilt swings by tens of degrees within decades. In others it shifts only slightly over the same span."

These dramatic changes remain predictions from computer models rather than confirmed observations. However, scientists may already have a way to look for evidence of them.

James Webb Telescope Could Reveal Magnetic Changes

Researchers are now examining detailed infrared images of Uranus and Neptune captured by the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST). They are particularly interested in the planets' auroras, which form when charged particles from the Sun interact with gases in their atmospheres.

Auroras are closely connected to planetary magnetic fields, making them valuable clues to what is happening far above the planets' hidden interiors. Changes in the appearance and arrangement of these glowing atmospheric features could help researchers determine whether the magnetic poles have shifted since Voyager 2's encounters.

"We are busy working on what's contained within these observations, but the shape and structure of the aurora, as well as other weird patterns in the ionosphere, promises to provide us with a way of understanding how the magnetic fields have changed, an important stepping stone," said Tom Stallard, a professor at Northumbria University who led the JWST observation campaign and reviewed the paper for the journal.

The ionosphere is a region of the upper atmosphere containing electrically charged particles. Its behavior is influenced by magnetic activity, offering another potential source of information about the planets' changing magnetic environments.

The research team hopes to combine Webb's auroral observations with Voyager 2's original magnetic field measurements and the new dynamo simulations. Together, these sources of evidence could provide the clearest picture yet of how the magnetic fields of Uranus and Neptune are arranged today.

Even so, scientists emphasize that fully understanding the planets' deep interiors will require new spacecraft missions to these distant worlds. Until then, improved simulations and powerful telescopes may help unravel one of the solar system's most unusual magnetic mysteries.

The research was funded by The University of Texas Institute for Geophysics (UTIG) in the Jackson School of Geosciences, the National Aeronautics and Space Administration (NASA), and the Science and Technology Facilities Council.

#581. FAA Grounds All US Flights Until Further Notice

Update, 8:45 pm EST Friday: The FAA’s ground stop is now over.

The Federal Aviation Administration grounded all US flights Friday afternoon, due to “extraordinary circumstances and for reasons of safety,” the agency said in a statement. Flights already in the air will be allowed to land, the agency said, but no new ones are permitted to take off.

In a post on X, US Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said that the ground stop was related to a communication issue affecting Air Traffic Control Centers and agency technicians are investigating.

The ground stop came after widespread reports of radio frequency outages among several of the nation’s most critical Air Traffic Control Centers, including in Boston, Chicago, and New York. Air traffic controllers use designated radio frequencies to communicate with pilots and manage and monitor airspace safety. In the past, controllers have used voice communications relayed over these radio frequencies to direct traffic; in recent years, the FAA has also rolled out a text-based communication system.

Reached for comment, an FAA spokesperson did not provide any additional details.

Nationwide ground stops are rare. The FAA halted all air traffic on September 11, 2001. In January of 2023, a ground stop was issued after an outage to a critical system that the FAA used to distribute real-time data and warnings to pilots, known as NOTAM (Notice to Air Mission).

“This really points to how much we need to modernize our air traffic control system,” says Susan Hotle, a professor of transportation engineering at Virginia Tech who worked at the FAA. “Right now all it takes is for one system to go out, and it affects a wide region at once.”

Earlier Friday, Duffy wrote congressional leaders to ask lawmakers to “act swiftly” on the Transportation Department’s request for $30.1 billion funding updates to the nation’s air traffic control system.

If the outage lasts more than a couple hours, it will take airlines—and the flyers that depend on them—days to recover, says Michael McCormick, a professor at Embry-Riddle Aeronautical University and a former air traffic controller. An extended stop would mean “airline systems are going to be complete in chaos, with aircraft and air crews in the wrong places. It would take days to get up and back running normally,” he says.

The FAA said this week that it was rolling out a new AI tool to help advise air traffic controllers. The tool, now available nationwide, had been tested in the busy Washington, DC, region. Air Space Intelligence, the FAA contractor responsible for the tool, posted on X that the groundstop “is not based on or a result of the SMART system, any product of ASI or our integration with FAA and [Air Traffic Control].”

At San Francisco International Airport on Friday, hoards of confused travelers lined up to speak with airline staff. A number flights at the airport had already been delayed because of high winds, and the FAA ground stop was now compounding the issue.

“The FAA has issued a nationwide ground stop,” a United Airlines employee said over the intercom at Terminal 3 near the E departure gates. “This is a nationwide event.”

Louise Matsakis contributed reporting from SFO.

#578. An ancient virus may help explain what makes the human brain different

A virus that infected chimpanzees millions of years ago has left behind a genetic legacy that may help explain an important difference between human and chimpanzee brain development. Researchers at Lund University have discovered that ancient viral DNA embedded in the chimpanzee genome switches off a gene that remains active in humans and appears to play an important role in the developing brain.

The findings, published in Science Advances, reveal how infections from the distant past can leave lasting changes in the genetic makeup of an entire population. They also offer new clues about how the brains of humans and our closest evolutionary relatives may have developed differently.

Although pandemics are often considered a modern threat, infectious diseases have been spreading through large animal and human populations for millions of years. Some of these ancient infections left traces of viral DNA that continue to influence how genes function today.

Johan Jakobsson, Professor of Molecular Neurogenetics at Lund University, says the discovery demonstrates how a virus can introduce genetic changes that eventually become widespread throughout a population.

"The virus we investigated specifically affected chimpanzees. Yet humans and chimpanzees have largely the same genetic makeup. That is why it is important to understand the differences in how genes are regulated. Our findings suggest that one gene, LINC00662, plays a significant role in early human brain development," says Johan Jakobsson.

How an Ancient Virus Silenced a Brain Development Gene

At the center of the discovery is a gene called LINC00662. Researchers found that it is active during early human brain development but completely switched off in comparable chimpanzee brain models.

The difference appears to be caused by a genetic remnant of PTERV1, an ancient virus that infected chimpanzees millions of years ago. PTERV1 belongs to a group known as endogenous retroviruses, which are viruses whose genetic material became incorporated into the DNA of their hosts. Once embedded in the genome, these viral sequences can be inherited by future generations.

Over time, PTERV1 produced numerous copies of its genetic material that became scattered across different parts of the chimpanzee genome. Researchers found that these viral sequences are systematically silenced through a process called methylation.

Methylation involves chemical modifications to DNA that help regulate gene activity. It is one of the ways cells control which genetic instructions are used and which remain inactive. In this case, the ancient viral insertion appears to have caused a neighboring gene involved in brain development to be switched off.

Jakobsson says understanding these differences in gene regulation could also provide important insights into how genetic activity protects the brain or contributes to damage and disease.

Miniature Brains Reveal a Striking Genetic Difference

To investigate the virus's effects, researchers grew miniature brain models called organoids using stem cells from humans and chimpanzees. These three-dimensional structures reproduce certain features of developing brain tissue, allowing scientists to study aspects of early brain development under controlled laboratory conditions.

When the team compared the two types of organoids, the difference was striking. LINC00662 showed strong activity in the human brain models but was completely inactive in those derived from chimpanzee cells.

"The gene was strongly expressed in the human organoids, but not at all in the chimpanzee organoids," says Patricia Gerdes, postdoc in molecular neurogenetics at Lund University.

The researchers then set out to determine whether the ancient viral DNA was directly responsible for this difference.

Using CRISPR-Cas9, a powerful gene editing technology that allows scientists to make targeted changes to DNA, they removed the viral sequence from chimpanzee cells.

The result provided compelling evidence. Once the viral DNA was removed, the previously inactive LINC00662 gene switched back on.

"This shows that it is in fact the viral insertion that is responsible for the gene being switched off in chimpanzees," says Patricia Gerdes.

The team also wanted to understand what LINC00662 actually does in the human brain. To investigate, they used CRISPR to switch off the gene in human brain organoids and examined how other genes responded.

Disabling LINC00662 reduced the activity of genes associated with nerve cell development and the formation of connections between neurons. These processes are essential for establishing the networks that allow the brain to communicate and function.

The results suggest that LINC00662 contributes to important stages of early human brain development, while the ancient viral insertion prevents the gene from functioning in chimpanzees.

More broadly, the discovery highlights an important principle of evolution. Although humans and chimpanzees share much of their genetic material, differences in how genes are switched on and off can influence how their bodies and brains develop.

The findings suggest that ancient viral infections may have contributed to these differences by altering gene regulation long after the original infections disappeared.

Researchers now plan to investigate whether other remnants of ancient viruses have similarly influenced brain evolution. They also hope to determine whether these inherited viral sequences may play a role in neurological diseases.

Together, the discoveries raise an intriguing possibility that some of the differences between human and chimpanzee brains may trace back, at least in part, to viruses that infected our evolutionary relatives millions of years ago.

#576. Physicists defy expectations by controlling the magnetic heart of a single atom

Researchers at Delft University of Technology (The Netherlands) have accomplished something that physicists previously thought should not be possible. Using radio waves, they managed to directly control the magnetic spin of the nucleus inside a single atom. The unexpected result challenges existing assumptions about how atomic magnetism behaves and could eventually lead to new ways of storing and retrieving quantum information.

The findings, published in Nature Communications, also present scientists with a new puzzle. Although the experiment demonstrated that this remarkable level of control is possible, researchers do not yet fully understand the mechanism that makes it work.

Atoms in magnetic metals such as iron and nickel behave like tiny magnets. However, the magnetism within an atom comes from different sources. Both electrons and, in some cases, the atomic nucleus possess a quantum property called spin, which can be thought of as a miniature magnetic compass needle.

"By far the strongest magnetism comes from the electrons," says lead researcher Sander Otte. "But in some atoms, the nucleus also carries a spin. That spin is much weaker, but it is also much better shielded from the outside world."

That protection makes nuclear spin especially interesting to quantum researchers. Because it is less exposed to disturbances from its surroundings, it could offer a promising way to preserve delicate quantum information.

To investigate these tiny magnetic properties, Otte's team uses a specially modified scanning tunneling microscope. This powerful instrument allows the researchers to deliver electrical signals to individual atoms with extraordinary precision.

Until recently, their experiments concentrated primarily on electron spin because it is easier to detect and measure. The nucleus's magnetic spin could only be observed indirectly through a phenomenon known as the hyperfine interaction, which creates a subtle quantum connection between the spins of the electron and nucleus. This connection can cause the two spins to become slightly entangled, meaning their quantum states are linked.

An Atomic Experiment Produces an Unexpected Result

In the latest study, lead author Hester Vennema and her colleagues took a different approach. They simultaneously applied two separate signals to a single atom, with one directed at the electron and the other at the nucleus.

By using these signals together, the researchers could investigate their effects independently. What happened next caught them by surprise.

Even when the electron and nuclear spins were largely disentangled, the team was still able to flip the orientation of the nucleus's magnetic spin. Based on their previous understanding, the nucleus should not have responded in this way.

"According to our simple understanding, this should not really happen," says Otte. "The nuclear spin should not be sensitive at all to the electrical signals from a scanning tunneling microscope."

The result suggests that the interaction between electrical signals and nuclear spin involves a mechanism that scientists have yet to fully explain.

Controlling Quantum Spins Like Guitar Strings

Achieving this level of precision required the researchers to adapt a technique called double magnetic resonance so it could operate on a single atom. The method allowed them to manipulate the magnetic behavior of the atomic nucleus separately from that of an electron.

Magnetic resonance works by applying a wave signal at a carefully selected frequency to change the orientation of a magnetic spin. However, quantum mechanics restricts these changes to specific allowed transitions, meaning the frequency must be precisely matched to the spin being targeted.

Vennema compares the process to playing a musical instrument.

"You can compare it to the strings of a guitar," Vennema explains. "Each possible spin rotation is like a string with its own note. You have to play precisely the right note to make a particular string vibrate. The "notes" of a nuclear spin are a hundred to a thousand times lower than those of an electron spin."

In other words, electrons and atomic nuclei respond to very different frequencies, much as different guitar strings produce different musical notes. By tuning their signals to the appropriate frequencies, the scientists could address the two types of spin separately.

A Quantum Breakthrough With a Mystery Still to Solve

The discovery could have important implications for the future of quantum information technology. Unlike conventional computers, which store information as bits representing either 0 or 1, quantum systems can use quantum states to encode information in more complex ways.

One major challenge is keeping those states stable long enough for information to be stored and retrieved reliably. Nuclear spins are attractive candidates for quantum memory because they are relatively well isolated from environmental interference.

The ability to control these spins directly could therefore open new possibilities for writing, storing, and reading quantum information. However, the findings represent an early experimental advance rather than a ready-to-use quantum memory technology.

For now, one of the most important outcomes is the new scientific mystery the experiment has revealed. The researchers have demonstrated an unexpected way to manipulate nuclear spin, but the physical mechanism responsible remains unclear.

"Back to the drawing board, then," says Otte. "That is really how science works. There is always something new left to figure out."

#575. Cosm rides premium moviegoing wave with more immersive screenings and a wider library

The next big thing in premium large format theatrical viewing takes inspiration from the stars.

Cosm, an immersive entertainment technology company, got its start programming planetariums — its name a combination of "cosmos" and "colosseum." The company's massive, domed screens first offered guests a view of the universe, now they mimic courtside seats at sports venues around the world and drop film fans into some of Hollywood's most iconic sets.

At a time when the theatrical industry is seeing its biggest growth from the sale of premium large format, or PLF, tickets, Cosm seeks to offer a fresh take on an elevated viewing experience. And it's quickly expanding both its footprint and its content library.

Since 2024, Cosm has opened four theaters — near Los Angeles, Dallas, Atlanta and Detroit — with a Cleveland hub set to open in 2027. These experimental venues have garnered the attention of sports fans, screening live matchups in an arena-like environment. Showings often sell out, according to Cosm.

More recently, Cosm has partnered with Hollywood studios like Warner Bros., now a part of Skydance, to bring the likes of "The Matrix," "Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory," "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" and "It" to its locations. Cosm recently signed a deal with Universal, which will bring "Shrek" to its domes in December.

The agreements mark a lucrative new chapter for the privately held Cosm, expanding its audience reach and viewing opportunities beyond timed, live events.

As a privately held company, Cosm doesn't disclose its financials. Tickets cost anywhere from $18 for a matinee to around $70 for prime seats or in-demand showings. That doesn't include added fees for concessions or items from special, themed menus tied to screenings.

Sony invested $100 million in the company in June, taking a minority stake and placing Sony Pictures Entertainment Chairman and CEO Ravi Ahuja onto its board of directors.

Cosm also counts among its investors Cosm chairman Steve Winn's firm, Mirasol Capital; former Milwaukee Bucks co-owner Marc Lasry's Avenue Sports Fund; Rocket Mortgage co-founder Dan Gilbert's Rock; and David Blitzer, managing partner of the New Jersey Devils and Philadelphia 76ers, who invested through his firm Bolt Ventures.

"Cosm is really early in its growth trajectory at the moment, and we saw an opportunity to partner with the company at, what I would say is at, a very important stage in its development," Jay Levine, chief strategy and business operations officer at Sony, told CNBC. "They have about five locations with the goal of a pretty significant rollout. And the real reason why we did it was it gave us a lot of exposure to a growing distribution and what we deem an experiential platform that we think has long-term potential."

Sony has made several plays in the theatrical space in recent years. It bought Alamo Drafthouse in 2024 and entered a long-term lease to reopen and restore the iconic Cinerama Dome in Los Angeles in July of this year.

"Our experiences strategy is around tech, it's around fandom, and it's around new and innovative approaches to content distribution," Levine said. "For us, we're all about these differentiated experiences."

Cosm's 87-foot-diameter, concave screens drop viewers into the middle of the action, whether it's an NFL game or inside the world of "The Matrix." The company's forthcoming Cleveland location will have an even bigger dome, at 100 feet in diameter.

The experience is akin to that of the Sphere in Las Vegas, which has garnered headlines since opening in 2023 for hosting high-profile concerts and events.

"People absolutely love to live out what they love in fandom, just like wearing a sports jersey," Alexis Scalice, head of Cosm Studios, told CNBC. "And I love that I'm seeing that same behavior happen with cinema."

Stadium-style seating is nestled within each Cosm dome, with booths and tables set on each level. The dome's width is such that it fills much of the viewer's periphery, giving the sensation that they are part of the on-screen environment.

The Los Angeles and Dallas locations have capacity for around 350 seats each, while the newer domes in Atlanta and Detroit have up to 500 available seats. Cosm has not shared how many seats the Cleveland location will have, but with its larger screen it is expected to have a larger number of seats.

For live sports events, Cosm has specialty cameras mounted in arenas that broadcast back to the dome, creating the illusion of being on the field of play.

For theatrical features, the film is played in a large rectangle in the center of the dome and the background is transformed to extend the scene.

In Cosm's presentation of "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone," for example, during the iconic scene where thousands of Hogwarts acceptance letters flood through the fireplace and into a living room, the background is swarmed with animated envelopes.

Cosm has partnered with experiential production company Little Cinema and creative studio MakeMake to create the visuals and environments for each scene of a film released in the dome. From storyboarding to post-production, this process can take up to a year as Cosm, the participating studio and the visual effects artists craft and render each moment.

"We really work hard not to take away from the story, so the film remains the hero in the narrative," Scalice said. "Working very closely with Warner Bros. and our creative partners to make sure that we're not detracting too much from the story, but we're enhancing those moments for more elevated emotional response."

With the exception of "It," which was released in 2017, Cosm's theatrical offerings thus far have been films that were released decades ago and that typically have long-established fan bases.

That helps Cosm leverage what Scalice called "that nostalgia factor" — "parents coming in that ... grew up on this, and they want to share it with their children."

"We can reinvigorate these legendary films," she added.

Cosm has dabbled across genres — horror, fantasy, science fiction — and also demographics — family-friendly and R-rated. "Shrek," set to debut Dec. 16, will be its first foray into animation.

"The thing that's so incredible about it is everybody thought initially it's just going to be working for sports, and when you look at what they've done, a lot of the titles with 'Harry Potter,' with 'Shrek,' and some of the other ones, I mean, the fill rates are actually incredible," Sony's Levine said.

Studios and theatrical exhibitors alike are looking for innovative ways to showcase Hollywood's product to moviegoers, especially as major shifts in viewing habits have disrupted the business.

In the post-pandemic era, the cinema space has been defined by fewer screens and fewer moviegoers, but those declines have been masked by higher movie ticket prices, particularly for PLF experiences.

These elevated offerings — think Imax, Dolby Cinema, ScreenX and 4DX — have bigger screens and better sound or introduce a unique experience, such as moving seats and fog machines. They're particularly popular with Gen Z and millennials, who've yet to be deterred by rising average ticket prices.

While an average standard movie ticket costs around $13, premium tickets average more than $18 each, according to data from EntTelligence.

Prices for Cosm's events fluctuate based on showtime and venue. Sony's Levine said the company's ticket prices and attendance rates are "higher than I think anybody really would have thought."

Unlike Cosm's sports offerings, which can also be viewed from outside the dome from its hall and deck viewing areas at a lower price, theatrical presentations are only available in the dome.

"They have no problem selling these tickets because people want to experience these things, like an immersive format," said Mike Polydoros, CEO at cinema marketing firm PaperAirplane Media. "That is something they can't get at home."

#573. The FAA Nationwide Ground Stop Is Over

Instability in a system that allows communications between the Federal Aviation Administration, air traffic controllers, and pilots was responsible for the country’s most disastrous nationwide ground stop in air traffic since September 11. The ground stop lasted five hours.

Several outlets reported that a failure in the FAA Telecommunications Infrastructure (FTI) network was linked to the FAA’s decision to declare a ground stop. The FTI is the private communications backbone for the air traffic control system. The system is in the midst of a major, multiyear modernization project, which involves replacing a decades-old network of copper wire with high-speed fiber-optic cables. The FAA has said the update will reduce outages and outage-related delays, and make the system safer. It will also be equipped to more easily handle newer aircraft, including drones, electric vertical take-off and landing aircraft (eVTOLs), and other “air taxis.”

At the time the ground stop was lifted on Friday evening, over 6,300 flights within, into, or out of the US had been canceled, according to the aviation tech company FlightAware; 9,500 had been delayed. Freight and mail flights have also been affected. It will likely take days, if not weeks, for airlines to reposition aircraft and crew and get their schedules back on track.

“It will take us some time to unwind what has happened,” US transportation secretary Sean Duffy said in a Friday evening briefing at the White House. He blamed “old switches, old routers,” and a “data overload” for the communications system failure. He said a “legacy system”—likely referring to FTI—was involved.

The issue was caused by “putting patches into the system” as new equipment was being installed, US Department of Homeland Security secretary Markwayne Mullin said at the White House briefing. “The new technology was overwhelming the older system,” he said.

Mullin said there were no indications that the outage was related to a cyberattack, but “there’s not complete certainty right now.”

The system is managed by the government contractor L3Harris Technologies. On its website, L3Harris says that its “team’s deep knowledge of the US national airspace allows us to upgrade systems without disrupting air traffic operations.” A spokesperson for the company did not respond to a request for comment.

In May, L3Harris said it had completed 50 percent of its modernization project. Seventy percent of FAA sites have been upgraded to fiber, the company said earlier this month. The Trump administration has said it aims to overhaul the nation’s air traffic control system by the end of 2028.

In August, the FAA tapped AT&T to upgrade the FTI, the private FAA communication network, to an entirely new system. That contract is worth $73 million, though its worth is likely to climb into the billions over the years. Previously, Verizon had been selected to build and operate this new system. L3Harris is meant to operate the legacy system until the upgrades are in place.

Earlier Friday, before the ground stop, US transportation secretary Sean Duffy sent a letter to congressional leaders asking them to approve his department’s request for $30.1 billion in funding to further upgrade the air traffic control system and to improve airports. On Friday, he upped the ask: “I want $50 billion,” he said during a briefing at the White House.

A September report published by the US Government Accountability Office raised concerns that the FAA’s attempt to accelerate long-delayed modernization plans “could be prioritizing speed over costs and air traffic disruption.” It found that the FAA does not have a “well-documented” life-cycle cost estimate for its programs, and that, rather than a master schedule guiding the sprawling project, has 11,389 schedules that are not integrated.

#571. Vitamin C could help block cancer-linked chemicals in your stomach, study finds

Vitamin C may do more than support everyday health. New research from the University of Waterloo suggests that it could also help prevent potentially cancer-causing chemicals from forming during digestion. Using mathematical modeling, scientists investigated how vitamin C influences chemical reactions in the digestive system that have been linked to cancer risk.

Exposure to nitrates and nitrites has increased in North American diets over the past several decades. These compounds are found in cured meats such as bacon and salami, as well as in fruits and vegetables grown in soil or water affected by pollution. Although nitrates and nitrites contribute to important functions in the brain and cardiovascular system, they can also participate in potentially harmful reactions once they reach the stomach.

One of these reactions, called nitrosation, can produce substances that researchers suspect may contribute to cancer development. Understanding what causes these chemicals to form, and what might prevent their formation, has been a longstanding challenge.

Why Vitamin C Could Change the Cancer Risk Equation

"Since at least the 90s, researchers have been studying the link between cancer and these compounds, with conflicting results," said Dr. Gordon McNicol, a post-doctoral researcher in applied mathematics and the first author of the study. "Our work suggests that the presence of dietary Vitamin C may help explain these inconsistencies."

To investigate this possibility, the researchers developed a mathematical model that simulates how nitrates and nitrites travel through the body and undergo chemical changes. The model incorporated several interconnected systems, including the salivary glands, stomach, small intestine, and plasma, the liquid portion of blood.

Their simulations revealed that vitamin C consumed alongside nitrates and nitrites may interfere with the formation of potentially harmful compounds. This could help explain why foods containing nitrates do not necessarily carry the same risks.

For example, leafy vegetables such as spinach naturally contain both nitrates and vitamin C. According to the model, the presence of vitamin C could limit the chemical reactions associated with cancer risk, potentially helping explain the protective effects suggested by some dietary research.

The researchers also explored whether vitamin C supplements might offer similar benefits when taken after eating.

Their findings suggest that taking vitamin C after each meal could moderately reduce the formation of nitrosation products associated with cancer risk. This effect could be particularly relevant when consuming foods containing nitrates and nitrites, including processed meats such as bacon and salami.

However, the results come from mathematical simulations rather than clinical trials. Although the model predicts that vitamin C can reduce the production of potentially harmful chemicals, further research is needed to determine whether this translates into a measurable reduction in cancer risk in humans.

New Clues for Future Cancer Prevention Research

The researchers believe their model could help scientists better understand the complicated relationship between diet, digestive chemistry, and cancer risk. It may also provide guidance for future experiments examining how nutritional choices influence the formation of potentially harmful compounds.

"This work provides a mechanistic roadmap for future clinical and laboratory studies by identifying the key interacting drivers of these potentially harmful chemical reactions, including nitrite exposure, antioxidant intake, meal timing, gastric conditions and oral microbiome activity," said Dr. Anita Layton, professor of applied mathematics and Canada 150 researcher chair. "This model can help researchers design more targeted experiments and interventions, focusing on when and in whom nitrosation is most likely to occur."

By identifying how factors such as vitamin C intake, meal timing, stomach conditions, and bacteria in the mouth interact, the model could help researchers develop more precise strategies for studying and potentially reducing cancer-related chemical reactions during digestion.

The study, "Vitamin C as a nitrosation inhibitor: A modelling study across dietary patterns and water quality," was published in the Journal of Theoretical Biology.

Materials provided by University of Waterloo. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
