#567. A hidden biological divide may help explain Alzheimer’s and chronic pain

Emerging research suggests that biological sex and genetics may play a major role in how neurological disorders develop, progress, and respond to treatment. The findings, which will be presented at Neuroscience 2026, reveal important differences that could help researchers develop more personalized approaches to treating complex brain conditions. Neuroscience 2026 is the annual meeting of the Society for Neuroscience and the world's largest source of emerging news about brain science and health.

Researchers are increasingly finding that certain neurological conditions can behave differently depending on a person's biological sex and genetic makeup. Understanding these differences could lead to treatments better suited to each individual's biology. Two conditions receiving particular attention are Alzheimer's disease (AD), an incurable disorder that progressively damages memory and other brain functions, and fibromyalgia, a chronic illness marked by persistent pain and fatigue that affects 2-4% of the global population. According to the World Health Organization, Alzheimer's affects between 35 and 40 million people worldwide.

Menopause, Genetics, and Alzheimer's Disease

Two of the new studies highlight how menopause and inherited genetic factors may influence Alzheimer's vulnerability and treatment responses.

One investigation found that people carrying genetic risk factors for Alzheimer's responded differently to menopause hormone therapy (MHT) compared with those without the same genetic risks. The effects also varied according to the particular combination of hormones used. These findings suggest that both genetic background and hormone therapy formulation deserve consideration when evaluating treatment options. (Mateja Perović, University of Toronto)

A separate study examined rodents undergoing menopause that also carried genetic risk factors for Alzheimer's. Researchers observed that brain circuits responsible for memory became particularly vulnerable, providing evidence that menopause may represent an important period of increased susceptibility for women who are genetically predisposed to the disease. (Stephanie M. Perez, University of Texas Health Science Center at San Antonio)

Sex Differences in Chronic Pain and Brain Inflammation

The other two studies explored how biological sex influences pain sensitivity, immune activity, and the effects of inflammation on the brain.

In a mouse model of fibromyalgia, females continued to show heightened sensitivity to touch and stronger immune system activation compared with males. The findings suggest that the biological processes contributing to chronic pain may differ between sexes, potentially making treatments tailored to these differences more effective. (Yasmin Sahlloul, University of Cincinnati)

Another investigation uncovered differences in how prolonged intestinal inflammation influences the brain. Using a mouse model, researchers found that chronic gut inflammation affected brain function and Alzheimer's related disease changes differently in males and females. The results provide additional evidence that biological sex may influence the relationship between inflammation and neurological disease. (Nurcan Kilic, University of North Dakota)

Toward More Personalized Treatments for Brain Disorders

Together, the four studies underscore the importance of examining sex and genetic differences when investigating neurological conditions. By identifying biological factors that influence disease progression and treatment responses, researchers hope to move toward more precise therapies for Alzheimer's, fibromyalgia, and other difficult-to-treat brain disorders.

"These four studies illustrate the fascinating discoveries that can be made when focusing on female-specific biology or possible sex differences in AD-related symptoms and risk factors," said Liisa Galea, PhD, Treliving Family Chair in Women's Mental Health at Centre for Addiction and Mental Health and moderator of the press conference. "The research presented at Neuroscience 2026 helps us pinpoint the specific differences that may help us develop more targeted treatment using precision approaches, even for some of our most complex brain disorders."

Materials provided by Society for Neuroscience. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#563. NASA is offering $1 million for bold ideas to help build a Moon base

NASA has launched a $1 million Moon Base Challenge, inviting teams of students from high schools and colleges across the United States to help develop ideas for humanity's future on the Moon. The competition was announced during a MAX POWER event at Kennedy Space Center Visitor Complex in Florida. Presented as part of a Golden Age of Science, the initiative gives young innovators an opportunity to contribute designs and potential solutions for NASA's planned lunar base.

"We're looking for students ready to think creatively, work as a team, and take on real problems connected to NASA's future on the Moon," said NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman. "You do not have to wait until graduation to contribute. Curiosity, creativity, and the willingness to tackle hard problems without obvious answers are exactly the qualities we need as we build what comes next."

NASA is working toward establishing a Moon Base near the lunar South Pole, where astronauts will be able to live, conduct research, and explore the surrounding region. Creating a lasting human presence on the Moon is also an important step toward future missions that will carry astronauts to Mars. Achieving these goals will require expertise and creative thinking from people across a wide range of industries and disciplines.

"As we build and design the foundations of Moon Base, we want to leverage all the ingenuity and innovation to think of every consideration our explorers might need to live and work there," said Carlos García-Galán, manager, Moon Base Program. "Students bring exactly the bold ideas that we're looking for, and I'm looking forward to their fresh perspective. Ultimately, Moon Base is for them, people who will experience a world where humans are on the Moon to stay."

Six Major Challenges of Living on the Moon

The competition will unfold in multiple phases, with student teams developing solutions to six essential aspects of operating a lunar base. These include generating and storing power, constructing facilities, maintaining life support systems, enabling transportation across the lunar surface, establishing communications networks, and protecting astronauts.

Each category addresses practical obstacles that NASA must overcome to support long-term operations on the Moon. By working on these problems, students can explore new approaches to lunar technologies while developing technical and professional skills that could prepare them for careers in aerospace and related fields.

The challenge is designed around actual environmental conditions and mission requirements that would affect daily life and operations on the lunar surface. Participants will produce work similar to that used in the space industry, including evaluations of competing technical options, proposals outlining potential designs, and presentations for project stakeholders.

Students from technical, vocational, and other academic backgrounds are encouraged to participate. Previous specialized knowledge of engineering or related technical fields is not required, making the competition accessible to a broad range of young innovators.

Inspiring the Next Generation of Space Explorers

Beyond generating ideas for the Moon Base, NASA hopes the competition will help develop the skills needed by the next generation of aerospace professionals. By exposing students to practical design challenges, the agency aims to encourage collaboration, creative problem-solving, and greater involvement in future space exploration.

"Students are the next generation of explorers and innovators. Their unique perspectives are key to helping us envision a future away from Earth, and I'm excited to see the creativity and ingenuity spurred by this challenge," said Elaine Ho, associate administrator for the Office of STEM Engagement at NASA Headquarters.

How to Enter NASA's $1 Million Moon Base Challenge

Interested student teams must register through the NASA STEM Gateway. Registration remains open until January 25, 2027.

NASA's Office of STEM Engagement developed the competition in partnership with the agency's Moon Base Program. The Institute of Competition Sciences is responsible for administering the challenge under a contract with NASA's Center of Excellence for Collaborative Innovation, which operates within the Research and Technology Mission Directorate.

Materials provided by NASA. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#560. The secret to building Moon bases may be hiding in emergency blankets

Establishing a permanent human presence on the Moon will require more than rockets, habitats, and life support systems. Engineers must also find ways to protect astronauts and equipment from extreme temperatures that can swing from hundreds of degrees below zero to boiling hot. Reliable insulation will be essential, but keeping heat under control is only part of the problem.

Materials used in lunar construction must also survive transportation, installation, accidental impacts, and years of handling. Many of the best insulators are surprisingly delicate, making them poorly suited to the demanding conditions of a working Moon base.

Researchers at the University of Pennsylvania may have found an answer in an unexpectedly familiar material: Mylar. This lightweight plastic is used in everyday products such as food wrap and, when coated with aluminum, the reflective emergency blankets that help marathon runners retain body heat. By arranging hundreds of extremely thin Mylar sheets into carefully designed stacks, the team has created insulation that remains effective even after being bent or subjected to intense pressure.

The research, published in ACS Applied Materials & Interfaces, introduces a material known as hybrid layered insulating film stacks (HyLIFS). Its main advantage is the ability to combine two qualities that are difficult to achieve together: excellent thermal insulation and resistance to physical damage.

"These stacks of Mylar can bend, withstand substantial compression and maintain their insulating performance," says senior author Igor Bargatin, Associate Professor in Mechanical Engineering and Applied Mechanics (MEAM), "potentially allowing them to be packed, transported and survive rough treatment over many years of use."

If engineers can eventually manufacture the material on a large scale, it could be used to protect lunar habitats, spacesuits, and other equipment designed to function for extended periods in the vacuum of space.

"The combination of thermal and mechanical robustness is critical for long-term use on the Moon," notes first author Danielle R. Levin, a recent Penn doctoral graduate in chemistry, who also received a master's in MEAM. "This material could open a pathway to inexpensive, durable and reusable lunar insulation."

Designing insulation for the Moon involves a difficult balancing act. A material must slow the movement of heat through a vacuum while also remaining strong enough to withstand the stresses of everyday use.

Some existing materials are outstanding at preventing heat transfer but offer little protection against physical damage. Aerogels, for instance, consist mostly of empty space, which makes them extremely lightweight and effective at retaining heat. They have been used in applications ranging from industrial pipelines to NASA's Mars rovers. However, their delicate internal structure can make them vulnerable to impacts and compression.

Another widely used space technology, multilayer insulation, relies on reflective sheets separated by insulating spacers. It works particularly well at reducing heat transfer through radiation, but it is not intended to support substantial weight. Even routine contact can cause tearing or other damage.

Strengthening insulation often creates another problem: the changes that make it more durable can also allow more heat to pass through. For future lunar infrastructure, engineers need a material that can resist damage without sacrificing thermal performance.

"For lunar infrastructure, we would ideally like both," says Bargatin. "The ideal material is both resistant to damage and to the loss of heat."

How Ordinary Mylar Became Powerful Space Insulation

The Penn researchers focused on Mylar, a well-known plastic formally called biaxially oriented polyethylene terephthalate, or BoPET. Already used extensively in aerospace technology, the polymer is also found in the lightweight emergency blankets distributed after marathons. Adding a metallic coating such as aluminum allows Mylar to reflect infrared radiation, helping prevent heat from escaping.

Rather than relying on a single sheet, the researchers investigated what would happen if many thin layers were stacked together. They experimented with different film thicknesses and applied extremely thin coatings of aluminum oxide, also called alumina, to some samples to make the sheets stiffer.

Assembling the experimental stacks was surprisingly difficult. Each delicate film had to be positioned individually, requiring painstaking manual work.

"At the moment, the process is very time-consuming," says Levin. "We use two pairs of tweezers and small weights to handle and stack the delicate films. Even breathing near the stack can send the layers flying."

Despite the simplicity of the basic idea, testing revealed an impressive combination of properties. In a vacuum, the best stacks achieved thermal conductivity comparable to other advanced vacuum insulation materials while retaining their flexibility.

The researchers also tested the material under compression similar to what insulation could experience inside a lunar habitat, where the interior air pressure would push against walls exposed to the vacuum outside.

Additional mechanical testing showed that stacks coated with alumina could tolerate the greatest compressive stresses available with the laboratory equipment while maintaining their insulating performance. The results demonstrated that improving durability did not necessarily require giving up thermal protection.

Crushing Pressure Reveals Unexpected Durability

One of the most striking findings came when the team subjected the material to repeated compression. Even the researchers were surprised by how well the thin plastic layers held up.

The samples were compressed to approximately 20 megapascals, a pressure comparable to concentrating 200 pounds of force onto an area the size of a fingertip. Instead of testing the material only once, the researchers repeated the process 10 times.

"We were concerned about the material failing under such high compressive stress," recalls Levin. "But the stacks didn't survive just one high stress cycle -- they survived 10 in a row."

This resistance to damage could be especially valuable for lunar exploration. Every piece of equipment needed on the Moon must first endure the challenges of being packed, launched, transported, and installed. Insulation that can bend, withstand pressure, and potentially be reused would offer significant practical advantages.

Durability would remain important after construction is complete. Astronauts living and working inside a lunar habitat could accidentally strike or compress insulation during maintenance or ordinary activities.

"You could use these stacks to insulate a lunar habitation, but astronauts will inevitably have little accidents where they bump into them," notes Bargatin. "When we're talking about long-term habitation on the moon, it's really important to have tough materials that can last long enough."

Tiny Particles Reveal a Hidden Secret of Heat Transfer

Beyond demonstrating the material's durability, the experiments uncovered an important clue about how heat travels through layers of Mylar.

The discovery involved microscopic particles already present in commercially manufactured plastic films. Extremely thin sheets naturally tend to stick to one another and to nearby surfaces, which complicates manufacturing and handling. To prevent this, producers add tiny "antiblocking" particles that extend slightly above the plastic surface and keep neighboring sheets from clinging together.

Although manufacturers have long understood why these particles are useful, their influence on heat transfer in stacked Mylar films had received relatively little attention.

Traditional multilayer space insulation is primarily designed to control heat transferred by radiation. As a result, researchers have had less reason to investigate how small points of physical contact between films might affect heat conduction.

The Penn team examined this possibility by constructing mathematical models of heat transfer between individual layers and comparing the predictions with laboratory measurements.

The findings indicate that, in a vacuum, much of the heat moving from one Mylar sheet to the next passes through the tiny contact points created by the antiblocking particles.

That result could have important implications for future insulation design. Instead of accepting the particles as an incidental feature of commercial plastic, engineers might deliberately control their size, abundance, and placement to reduce unwanted heat transfer.

Why Thinner Plastic Does Not Always Insulate Better

The role of these microscopic particles also helped explain another unexpected result.

At first, the researchers assumed that thinner Mylar sheets would produce better insulation. Using thinner films would allow more layers to occupy the same amount of space, increasing the number of boundaries that heat must cross.

However, the experiments did not fully support that expectation. When coated with alumina, the thinnest films the team successfully characterized provided thermal performance similar to films of medium thickness.

The researchers suspect that the antiblocking particles help explain why. In thinner sheets, the particles can produce more contact between adjacent layers, creating additional routes for heat to move through the material. Those extra contact points can offset the expected advantages of adding more layers.

The finding suggests that making films thinner is not automatically the best approach. Engineers must also consider how the sheets bend and deform under pressure, along with the number and arrangement of microscopic contact points.

"As you compress the layers, the contact landscape changes," says Levin. "It's not just a matter of minimizing the film thickness or the number of particles; we must also account for how the contacts change and the films deform as pressure increases."

Ultimately, the ability of these stacks to limit heat transfer depends on several interacting factors, including the thickness and stiffness of the plastic and the distribution of particles within it.

From Tiny Laboratory Samples to Future Moon Bases

Although the early results are encouraging, the new insulation is still far from being ready for lunar construction.

The experimental HyLIFS samples currently measure only about one square centimeter, and producing them requires hours of careful cutting and manual stacking. That painstaking process would be impractical for manufacturing the large sheets needed to insulate an entire habitat.

To make the material useful on a larger scale, the researchers will need a more efficient production method. One possibility involves continuously applying coatings to large rolls of Mylar, using manufacturing techniques that could eventually produce sheets measured in meters rather than centimeters.

Such an approach might also allow engineers to adjust the thickness of the plastic, the protective coatings, and the distribution of antiblocking particles before assembling the material into larger stacks.

Important questions remain. The researchers still need to determine whether large versions of the material will retain the same insulating properties and mechanical strength seen in the laboratory. Further testing must also establish how well the stacks tolerate repeated bending, accidental impacts, scratches, and other wear associated with real-world use.

If those challenges can be overcome, the technology could provide lightweight, flexible, durable insulation for lunar habitats, rovers, electronic equipment, and other systems operating in space.

For Levin, the results also offer a broader lesson about materials engineering. Even familiar products can reveal unexpected capabilities when researchers examine how their structures behave under different conditions.

"The simplest configuration ended up being the best one," she says.

The research was carried out at the University of Pennsylvania School of Engineering and Applied Science (Penn Engineering). Additional co-authors include Matthew F. Campbell, Gulzhan Aldan, and Orevaoghene H. Omodior.

Funding was provided by the National Science Foundation Division of Chemical, Bioengineering, Environmental and Transport Systems (grant number 1845933) and Penn Engineering.

Materials provided by University of Pennsylvania School of Engineering and Applied Science. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#559. Poppi founders are going after PepsiCo's Gatorade with new beverage startup Jetty

After disrupting the soda category with Poppi, founders Allison and Stephen Ellsworth are now taking aim at hydration with their new brand, Jetty.

Starting Monday, Jetty drinks and ready-to-mix electrolyte packets will be on shelves at Whole Foods grocery stores and available online at Target and Amazon.

The new, self-funded venture directly pits the Ellsworths against PepsiCo, which bought Poppi for $1.95 billion in 2025. Pepsi owns Gatorade, which dominates the U.S. sports drink category with about 60% market share, according to Numerator data.

Poppi shook up the soda category with prebiotic fiber, low calorie and sugar counts and its fun marketing aimed at Generation Z and millennials. Jetty looks to upend the hydration category through a similar strategy. To do so, it will face off not only against Gatorade but also a swarm of other upstarts looking to get in on the electrolyte-fueled trend.

For decades, Gatorade and other sports drinks have pitched their products to athletes who need electrolytes and fluids after an intense workout, although many consumers drink them every day without even sweating. Jetty is aimed at people who need a hydration boost, but not the same level of electrolytes or sugar as a traditional sports drink.

"No one has really created a product for just everyday hydration — literally everyday hydration for normal people," Allison Ellsworth, Jetty's chief brand officer, told CNBC. "Like I woke up this morning, I didn't run a marathon."

Following Poppi's sale to PepsiCo, Ellsworth stayed on as an advisor to the brand, although she said she is no longer involved in the day-to-day operations.

After 10 years building Poppi, Ellsworth said, she finally had free time. As Ellsworth tells it, she went to the doctor for the first time in a decade. She started using a GLP-1 medication and lost 60 pounds.

Ellsworth's husband, Stephen, now CEO of the new brand, noticed she wasn't hydrating, even when she came back from the gym.

GLP-1 medications such as Ozempic and Mounjaro can make people less thirsty. But because staying hydrated can also help mitigate some of the drugs' side effects, such as nausea and headaches, it can become more important.

"I physically struggle getting liquid into my body," Ellsworth said.

Plain old water is often less appealing to consumers, even dehydrated GLP-1 users, but more flavorful options with added benefits can be tempting. About 44% of GLP-1 users report consuming more functional beverages, according to EY-Parthenon's third annual survey of more than 3,600 current or former GLP-1 users.

Ellsworth sees a broader appeal for Jetty, too.

"Our core audience is going to be millennial moms and Gen Z," she said. "Gen Z just isn't relating to these old school brands, the legacy of the Gatorades and the Powerades. They're just not cool."

Consumers broadly have become more interested in staying hydrated.

Dollar sales of hydration and electrolyte products climbed 20% to $2.6 billion over the 52 weeks ended Oct. 4, based on data from SPINS, which tracks new universal product codes, or UPCs. The category also has a strong repeat buyer rate of 62%.

However, the Ellsworths aren't the only ones to notice the potential in hydration. Big food and beverage companies have been looking to hydration, too.

Kraft Heinz debuted Capri Sun juice pouches and Kool-Aid packets with electrolytes this spring. In April, Pepsi unveiled an overhaul of the Gatorade brand, focused on making it more appealing to a wider swath of consumers, with more potential occasions like a "fresh morning start" or an "afternoon walk." And Electrolit is a top performer for its distributor Keurig Dr Pepper, with 40% retail sales growth in the company's third quarter.

All of that means more competition for shelf space and consumers' "share of throat," an industry term for beverage market share.

To increase Jetty's visibility across the grocery store and to maximize convenience for shoppers, Jetty will be available in both powders and bottles.

Single-use electrolyte mixes are growing at a much faster clip than the ready-to-drink category, Ellsworth said, thanks to Unilever's Liquid I.V. and smaller upstarts like LMNT. On Amazon alone, powders are a $1 billion category, according to Ellsworth.

The ready-to-drink, or RTD, sports beverages are a much larger market, but the category's sales are shrinking as consumers reach for other hydration options instead. Unit sales of sports drinks have fallen 9.1% from 2022 to 2026, according to Numerator data. And Coca-Cola has written down the value of BodyArmor by more than $1.7 billion after buying control of the brand for $5.6 billion in late 2021.

"Me and my husband know how to do RTD," Ellsworth said.

Jetty will launch with five flavors, including watermelon, orange mango, fruit punch, black raspberry and white cherry. All of them contain 100 milligrams of sodium, 310 milligrams of potassium and 100 milligrams of magnesium in a 15.2-ounce bottle or 15-gram packet.

In addition to electrolytes, Jetty products also contain B vitamins, 3 grams of fiber and 6 grams of sugar. It doesn't use artificial sweeteners or dyes, its founders say.

For comparison, a 20-ounce bottle of traditional Gatorade Thirst Quencher has about 270 milligrams of sodium and 80 milligrams of potassium, plus 34 grams of sugar. The sports drink brand is also phasing out artificial colors and promoting lower-sugar versions.

"What's really interesting about this product and this opportunity is we now can capture all those lapsed drinkers — or that generation that didn't even ever drink it in the first place," Ellsworth said.

Poppi's new owner knows about Jetty and the market it is trying to capture, too.

"I don't think [Pepsi] gets it, but what they do get is that the movement is changing, and they think that just by taking dyes out or doing 90% less sugar, they're going to capture this audience," Ellsworth said. "But that is very big corporate thinking."

She added that she believes Pepsi has missed the mark on advertising to Gen Z consumers across its portfolio, such as in its campaigns for Sprite competitor Starry.

Perhaps as an extension of that, Poppi's performance has been underwhelming under PepsiCo's ownership.

The food and beverage giant now estimates the fair value of potential performance-based payments to Poppi's prior owners is $12 million, Pepsi said in a regulatory filing in October. At the time of the deal, Pepsi promised an additional payment of $300 million if Poppi hit certain targets by the third quarter of 2027. The Ellsworths declined to comment on the filing to CNBC.

This time around, with one successful brand under their belts, Jetty's founders say they have learned from past mistakes. Those lessons should help Jetty as it launches at a much larger scale than Poppi did.

Three different claims lawyers have signed off on Jetty's packaging, according to Ellsworth. Poppi paid $8.9 million to settle class-action litigation alleging false advertising over its "gut healthy" claims.

For now, Jetty is entirely self-funded with $2.5 million, although Ellsworth said that the brand will likely raise money in 2027. This time, though, she said investors will probably look less like traditional venture capitalists.

"I want to bring in investors that bring value," Ellsworth said. Instead of a venture capitalist who is willing to invest $15 million, Jetty would rather consider investors who can only write a check for $1 million but have access to celebrities. "So then you're getting access to talent on a cheaper level and people that are invested in the brand."

And Jetty has also learned a few lessons from Gatorade. For example, the brand is already at work on a lemon-lime version — the original flavor of Gatorade.

"We'll eventually do it," Ellsworth said. "But it was really important at the beginning just to stick to flavors that we love, for it to taste good."

#556. NASA smashed into an asteroid. Now Hera has revealed a strange new world

The European Space Agency's (ESA) Hera spacecraft has captured its first close-up image of the Didymos asteroid system, offering scientists a surprising new perspective on the site of NASA's historic asteroid deflection experiment.

On September 26, 2022, NASA's DART spacecraft deliberately crashed into Dimorphos, the smaller of the two asteroids in the system. Hera's latest image provides a look at its larger companion, Didymos, from a side that scientists had not previously seen in such detail.

The image was taken from a distance of 60 km using Hera's monochromatic Asteroid Framing Camera, which captures images in black and white. Although most of Dimorphos, which measures 151 m across, is concealed in shadow, the much larger Didymos, with a diameter of 780 m, is clearly visible.

A Surprisingly Different Side of Asteroid Didymos

"The first thing we said was wow!" observes Hera Principal Investigator Patrick Michel, Director of Research at CNRS / Observatoire de la Côte d'Azur. "This is the other side of Didymos from the one that DART's camera showed us shortly before impact, so this feels like looking at a whole new world.

"This side of Didymos has the same central ridge along its equator that we saw before, but otherwise appears very different, with multiple boulders and craters and what look like patterns of grooves or ridges."

The newly revealed terrain raises intriguing questions about how the asteroid's surface developed and whether NASA's collision with Dimorphos may have left traces on its larger neighbor.

When DART struck Dimorphos, the impact sent material flying into space. Scientists believe some of that debris may have traveled across the roughly 1 km separating the two asteroids and reached Didymos. Determining whether this material contributed to the features now visible on its surface will be an important part of Hera's investigation.

ESA's Hera mission scientist Michael Kueppers adds: "Since we expect a lot of impact-ejected material to have made it across the approximately 1 km of space from Dimorphos to Didymos, it may be that some of these characteristics originate from this transport of material, and we have exciting work ahead of us to sort it out. We look forward to seeing much more of both bodies and systemically mapping all the changes we identify."

NASA's DART Mission Changed an Asteroid's Orbit

Although Dimorphos is largely obscured in Hera's new image, it holds a remarkable place in space exploration history. It became the first object in the Solar System to have its orbit measurably altered by human activity.

NASA achieved this milestone by sending the 580 kg DART spacecraft into Dimorphos at approximately 6.1 km/s. The collision reduced the time the smaller asteroid takes to complete an orbit around Didymos by about 33 minutes, give or take a minute. That change was substantially greater than scientists had anticipated.

The experiment demonstrated that striking an asteroid with a spacecraft can change its motion through space. This approach, known as kinetic impact deflection, could potentially be used to redirect a dangerous asteroid before it reaches Earth.

Hera Begins Its Investigation of NASA's Asteroid Impact

After a two-year journey, Hera has reached the double asteroid system, located 196.8 million km from Earth. The spacecraft is now preparing for a detailed 'crash site investigation' to examine the consequences of NASA's groundbreaking experiment.

While DART demonstrated that an asteroid's orbit could be changed through a deliberate collision, Hera will help scientists understand the physical processes behind the result. By examining both asteroids and documenting their characteristics, researchers hope to improve their ability to predict how future asteroid deflection attempts might work.

The findings could help transform kinetic impact deflection from a successful one-time demonstration into a dependable, repeatable planetary defense technique. Such a capability could prove critical if an asteroid were ever discovered on a collision course with Earth.

"Seeing this image is a hugely exciting moment for the whole Hera family," comments Hera mission manager Ian Carnelli. "It has taken years of hard work and dedication to reach this point - but we're motivated to know that the adventure is only beginning!"

The mission's next major reveal is scheduled for November 25, when detailed images of both Didymos and Dimorphos will be unveiled, giving scientists and the public a closer look at the asteroids involved in humanity's first successful asteroid deflection experiment.

Materials provided by European Space Agency (ESA). Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#552. Restaurant chains refocus on food quality to bring back diners

Juicier chicken tenders. Revamped sauces. Richer and creamier Frosties.

Across the restaurant industry, chains are turning their focus back to their food in the hopes that better quality will win back diners.

Over the last two years, consumers have dined out less frequently and grown pickier about where they choose to eat, based on data from Black Box Intelligence. In that time, "value" has been the buzzword du jour for restaurant executives, who have explained to analysts and investors that the word encompasses more than menu pricing alone.

Now, industry CEOs are turning to their corporate chefs to try to beat rivals on quality and taste, as they look to expand their definition of value. Discounts and one-off promotions can lift traffic for a time, but the actual product still matters for restaurants' long-term growth, as well as franchisee profitability.

From Wendy's to Chipotle Mexican Grill, chief executives touted new or upcoming improvements to menu items on their quarterly earnings conference calls in recent weeks.

"We think the product's improved and even better," Chipotle CEO Scott Boatwright told analysts on the company's call in late October, referring to the burrito chain's brisket, which returned to menus for a limited time Thursday.

For some chains, better food is a key step in ambitious turnaround plans.

For example, "quality food at real value" is one pillar of Wendy's five-part turnaround strategy that the chain unveiled on Thursday.

"We're also rebuilding our food offerings and have begun testing improvements to quality and craveability of our core items, including thicker, crispier bacon, a richer and creamier Frosty, whole leaf lettuce on sandwiches and improved salads," Wendy's CEO Bob Wright told analysts on the call Thursday.

That same day, the company reported its seventh straight quarter of same-store sales declines and publicly rejected a $1.4 billion takeover bid led by Nelson Peltz's Trian Partners. Wendy's said the proposal did not take into account the potential value that its turnaround plan could unlock.

There is precedent to suggest that better-tasting food can contribute to higher sales.

In 2009, Domino's Pizza changed its recipe, swapping out its sauce, adding garlic to its crust and switching to shredded mozzarella with a hint of provolone. Along with blunt commercials that compared its previous crust to cardboard and old sauce to ketchup, the changes helped to spur U.S. same-store sales growth of 9.9% the following year.

Now, some of the same team that led the Domino's turnaround is copying the playbook at Burger King's U.S. restaurants.

Part of the burger chain's multiyear comeback plan has focused on revamping its core menu items. It started earlier this year with its flagship offering, the Whopper, which received a better bun and a new mayonnaise. And in September, it rolled out an overhaul of its nuggets, switching to 100% white meat, crispier breading and a more flavorful marinade. It also reformulated its sauces.

"We've tried to look at our biggest categories first," Josh Kobza, CEO of Burger King parent Restaurant Brands International, told CNBC. "We've also thought about the ones where the fixes are more straightforward and less operationally involved."

Following the changes, Whopper sales were up by a double-digit percentage in the third quarter, Kobza said on the company's earnings call in late October. And nugget sales climbed more than 35%. Burger King said its U.S. same-store sales jumped 8.4% in the quarter.

Like Domino's, Burger King is highlighting diners' past gripes with its Whoppers and nuggets in its ads, which star Burger King U.S. president and former Domino's executive Tom Curtis.

Better-tasting food isn't just for chains that are in trouble.

Even Chili's, which has seen its same-store sales soar 52% over the past three years, is improving more of its existing menu options. After tackling its baby back ribs, burgers and nachos, it will turn next to salads and steaks, Brinker International CEO Kevin Hochman told CNBC.

"We're going to be doing more dry aging of our beef to make it more tender, versus doing it in a plant," he said. "It's a little bit more expensive, but we think the taste is going to be a whole lot better."

Chili's rival Applebee's launched a juicier chicken tender during its latest quarter, according to John Peyton, CEO of the chain's parent company Dine Brands. In 2027, Applebee's plans to overhaul more of its protein options.

"We think proteins are, number one, our biggest opportunity," Peyton told CNBC. "Number two, they're a big focus of the way people are thinking about eating right now, leaning into proteins and a little bit less with the carbs."

Chicken is also a focus for McDonald's, which is testing hand-breaded wings, strips and a sandwich in a handful of Chicago-area restaurants. It plans to expand the trial to more locations in the U.S. and Ireland in early 2027.

Moreover, McDonald's is trying to improve its coffee, with fresher beans and upgraded equipment, executives said at the company's investor day in September. Both chicken and coffee are fast-growing categories, offering more opportunities to lift sales than McDonald's signature beef burgers.

While the burger giant isn't in full turnaround mode like rivals Wendy's and Burger King, its U.S. business has struggled in recent quarters after its value strategy didn't gain the traction the company had hoped. In the third quarter, its U.S. same-store sales fell 0.2%.

In addition to menu tests, McDonald's is also leaning into training to improve the quality and consistency of its core menu items. Starting in October, the company launched "Make It Golden," a multiyear training program for corporate employees, restaurant workers and franchisees.

Ultimately, improved menu items mean little if the recipe changes aren't well executed.

#550. AdultFriendFinder vs. FetLife: Which is better for exploring specific kinks?

If you're new to exploring kinks and fetishes online, it can be a weird and intimidating experience. But it's also exciting, which is why it's worth exploring deeper.

What you need is the right platform to get started. There are great sites and apps out there that offer sexy fun with like-minded folk. It doesn't matter if you have specialist kinks for feet, spanking, or BDSM — or even a relatively vanilla taste for threesomes — you can find your tribe somewhere online.

Experienced fet-lovers will already know that regular dating apps just don't cut it. So, the question is, how do you connect with similarly-minded kinksters? Tinder — top pick for fast hookups

Hinge — popular choice for regular meetups

OkCupid — best app for inclusivity

We strongly recommend two platforms for exploring your favorite kinks and more. Those platforms are AdultFriendFinder and FetLife, both of which are stacked with NSFW content. Seriously, do not log on to these in public. And who knows? You might even discover a few kinks you never even knew you had.

Maybe you just don't know what you're into yet. But if you want to find a safe, non-judgmental space to explore your options and go on a journey of sexual self-discovery, these are the places to do it.

Before we get deep into the pros and cons of AFF and FetLife, let us share some general information with you. AdultFriendFinder and FetLife both skew male, which is fairly standard across the online dating spectrum. The average user on FetLife is aged 25, while AdultFriendFinder users tend to be in their 30s. Even so, both sites offer a diverse range of genders and ages — not to mention a colorful range of sexual orientations and kink communities. In other words, there really is something for everyone.

But while both sites are designed to accommodate sex-positive communities and make connections between kinksters, they have their own way of doing things. You may find that one site suits you better than the other, though we recommend trying both on for size to see which one fits.

Keep reading for a rundown of the major differences between AFF and FetLife and decide which one to try first.

FetLife is very popular, but be aware that it can take time to build a following and earn trust on the site. While it's possible to message strangers, the site culture discourages it. Don't be surprised if unsolicited messages are ignored completely. AdultFriendFinder, on the other hand, is a DM-sliding free-for-all. In fact, the concept of connecting with strangers is kind of the point. In that respect, AFF operates like a regular dating site (it's one of the OGs in that field, dating back to 1996), whereas FetLife is more of a social media website for building local kink communities.

AdultFriendFinder also hosts live webcam shows and community discussion forums, offering users multiple ways to connect quickly. There's more to it than just one-on-one.

This one might be controversial, since the AFF site is notoriously dated, but FetLife's interface is downright confusing, a mix between Facebook and Instagram, with much more limited search functionality. While both sites will allow you to search by specific kinks (and both sites offer exhaustive lists of kink options), the search functionality and filter options on AdultFriendFinder are more comprehensive and more user-friendly.

This isn't an accident, by the way. FetLife deliberately disables search by age, gender, and sexual orientation because, in their words, they are "a social network and not a dating site."

Remember that AFF looks and feels dated because it's been around for three decades and has kept things very old school indeed. AFF doesn't even have an app — it's website only. Be warned that the interface is chock-full of nudity and NSFW content.

FetLife allows users to host videos, but it doesn't have a dedicated, live webcam service. Some FetLife users will offer that, but they do this on third-party sites, and usually behind a paywall. AFF, on the other hand, makes live streaming a significant part of its community experience and allows you to search through webcam shows by the streamer's location or the show's interest/topic, which often revolves around a specific kink.

In other words, if you're content to watch, AFF delivers some excellent, kink-centric live action, and much of it is accessible without upgrading to a paid subscription.

The most noticeable difference between FetLife and AFF is how much more freedom is afforded to non-paying accounts. On AdultFriendFinder, profiles are locked behind a paywall, so only paying members can scroll through photos, view bios, and see a comprehensive list of kinks. FetLife, on the other hand, operates more like Instagram for kinksters. It delivers large photos, comprehensive bios, and even a web of connections comparable to what you might see on Facebook, from friends and romantic partners to subs, doms, and mentors, if applicable.

Even more important, free FetLife users can contact each other in one-on-one messages without having to give up their credit card information, which definitely makes it easier to establish a personal connection.

One major limitation of free accounts is that users can't watch videos. Because raunchy videos are a mainstay on many profiles, this is definitely a hindrance, though it's not a deal-breaker.

Perhaps the biggest benefit of FetLife over AdultFriendFinder is that the former is much more focused on real-life connections, whereas AdultFriendFinder seems content to let people play in the virtual world.

The Events tab at the top of the FetLife banner allows you to see, at a glance, all of the nearby, kink-friendly events, from informal meet-and-greets to full-on costume and masquerade balls. Best of all, you can filter by sub-type (Party, Educational, Social, Conference/Festival, Sex Party) or just look for events that your FetLife friends are attending.

These in-person events are integral to the FetLife experience, even when the events aren't officially affiliated with the site itself. In fact, it's pretty common to see people indicate, in their profiles, that they won't message or "friend" anyone they haven't already met at an IRL event.

Because of its community emphasis and dedication to exploring sexuality in a safe, consensual, and informed way, FetLife offers users not only the means to connect with others but also to better connect with themselves. There's the community-sourced Kinktionary, for example, a Wiki-like resource covering topical aspects of human sexuality from the ins and outs of gay culture to a comprehensive list of sex toys and their uses, as well as dedicated groups to not only explore kinks but also introduce people to the BDSM lifestyle (we recommend you check out the Novices & Newbies section if you're a first-time visitor).

AdultFriendFinder isn't totally without these resources, but they're not as user-friendly or as accessible to total newcomers to the world of kink.

Both AFF and FetLife are among the very best online destinations for kink-based fun. They have their pros, cons, and differences, but you're unlikely to go wrong with either. They each have large user bases and comprehensive selections of kink-focused spaces. That helps create a non-judgmental atmosphere, allowing you to let go of any sense of inhibition. Just let loose and enjoy yourself doing it.

That said, if you're looking for the fastest possible connection and you're happy to keep things strictly online for now, AdultFriendFinder is the place to start. If, on the other hand, you want to integrate into your local kink community and get on the guest lists of underground fetish clubs or VIP sex parties, FetLife can make that happen.

#541. Eli Lilly’s Foundayo obesity pill eases sleep apnea in late-stage trial, paving way for approval

Eli Lilly on Monday said its obesity pill Foundayo significantly reduced the severity of obstructive sleep apnea, the most common sleep-related breathing disorder, in patients with obesity, in a late-stage trial.

The drugmaker plans to file by the end of the year for U.S. approval of the daily pill for moderate-to-severe obstructive sleep apnea, or OSA, in adults with obesity or who are overweight. The results make the pill — which rolled out in the U.S. in April — one of several GLP-1 treatments to show promise in treating the condition.

OSA refers to interrupted breathing during sleep due to narrowed or blocked airways, which can cause drops in oxygen and increases in blood pressure that are associated with serious complications, such as hypertension, heart failure and stroke. The condition affects nearly 1 billion adults globally, including 80 million people ages 20 and over in the U.S., according to the National Council on Aging. Most have never been diagnosed, Lilly said in a release.

Lilly's blockbuster weight loss injection Zepbound became the first medication to win U.S. approval for OSA in December 2024. Patients have limited treatment options outside of that drug and many use cumbersome machines that provide positive airway pressure, or PAP, to allow for normal breathing.

"Foundayo is already making a difference for people living with obesity, and with plans to submit these results to the FDA by the end of the year, we could have the opportunity to help people living with OSA," Dr. Thomas Seck, senior vice president of product development of Lilly Cardiometabolic Health, said in a release.

The trial tested Foundayo in two independent studies, the first of which included 363 patients who were unable or unwilling to use PAP therapy. The second study included 349 people who were on PAP therapy for at least three months at the time of screening and continued that treatment during the trial.

Patients in both studies who took Foundayo started at a low dose and increased it gradually at four-week intervals to different maximum dosages.

Researchers specifically examined how much the pill reduced the so-called apnea-hypopnea index, or AHI, which records the number of times per hour a person's breathing shows a restricted or completely blocked airway. The index is used to evaluate the severity of obstructive sleep apnea and the effectiveness of treatments for the condition.

People who took Foundayo and were not using PAP therapy had an average of 21.1 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 4.8 per hour for the placebo, according to the results.

Foundayo also met the trial's second goal in that group, leading to an overall AHI reduction of 43.8% compared with a decrease of 6.3% for the placebo. Lilly said 31.5% of the participants in the Foundayo group met the criteria for remission or improvement to mild OSA.

Meanwhile, patients who were on both Foundayo and PAP therapy had an average of 18.3 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 5.5 per hour for the placebo.

Foundayo led to an overall AHI reduction of around 27.5% in that group, compared with a decrease of 3% for the placebo. The company said 19.6% of the participants on Foundayo and PAP therapy also met the criteria for remission or mild OSA.

Foundayo also reduced what's known as the sleep apnea-specific hypoxic burden — a measure of how deeply and for how long oxygen levels fall during sleep. That metric fell by 55.9% in people not using PAP therapy and 45.8% in those using it.

Lilly said the pill also helped patients lose roughly 11% of their weight across groups at 52 weeks and helped to lower a marker of inflammation.

The company said the safety data and how well patients tolerated Foundayo were generally consistent with previous trials on the pill.

Around 12% of people on Foundayo who were not using PAP therapy discontinued due to side effects, compared to 3.8% with the placebo. Meanwhile, 6.8% of those on both Lilly's pill and PAP discontinued the drug due to side effects, compared with 0.6% in the placebo group.

The most common side effects were gastrointestinal, including nausea, diarrhea, constipation and vomiting, and were generally mild to moderate in severity. That's consistent with other GLP-1 medications.

#532. Goodyear Tire CEO Mark Stewart steps down as company continues turnaround plan

Goodyear Tire & Rubber Co. announced Monday that CEO Mark Stewart would be stepping down from his role effective immediately.

Board member Roger Wood is taking over as interim CEO and president, Goodyear said.

The leadership change comes as the tire company is in the midst of a multiyear transformation plan.

Stewart was hired in January 2024 to lead the company, with the goal of revitalizing the legacy American business to make it more efficient and profitable.

The company has faced disappointing results thus far, reporting last week third-quarter results that saw net sales down 2.3% year over year and an adjusted net loss of $31 million. Goodyear failed to reach a 10% operating margin by the end of last year, which was a major goal of the turnaround plan.

Goodyear said it formed a special committee of the board earlier this year to conduct a "strategic review of the company's businesses and operations focused on identifying opportunities to strengthen performance and drive shareholder value."

It said Monday it will continue that work by evaluating strategic and operational initiatives that include further optimizing the company's business mix and footprint, improving efficiency and investing in new products and capabilities that support long-term growth.

"Goodyear has made meaningful progress in becoming a more efficient and focused company, and the board believes this is the right moment to transition leadership to accelerate that progress and capture additional opportunity," Goodyear Chair Joe Hinrichs said in a press release.

The company has faced headwinds from tariffs, higher raw material costs and increased competition from China.

Wood has been a member of the company's board of directors since July 2023 and was previously the co-CEO of Tenneco as well as CEO of Dana Holding Corporation.

The company will start a search for a permanent replacement as part of its strategic review.

Shares of Goodyear were down roughly 4% in early trading Monday.

#522. Honda to invest $3 billion in new vehicle assembly plant in Ohio

MARYSVILLE, Ohio — Honda Motor plans to invest $3 billion to build a new vehicle assembly plant in Ohio, the automaker announced Monday.

Honda said the new plant, which will be near its other facilities in Marysville, Ohio, is expected to begin production in 2029, have a capacity for 240,000 vehicles annually and create more than 2,000 jobs.

The facility is expected to be capable of producing traditional vehicles with internal combustion engines, all-electric vehicles and hybrid models, which have been growing in popularity in the U.S.

Honda declined to disclose what vehicles the company plans to produce at the new facility.

The plant is expected to increase Honda's light-duty vehicle capacity to 1.3 million units annually in the U.S. It currently has 12 manufacturing plants in United States, eight of which produce engines or light-duty vehicles for the auto industry.

"This is not simply an investment in another plant. It is an investment in the future of Honda manufacturing in America," Bob Nelson, executive vice president of American Honda Motor, said during the announcement at the company's U.S. heritage center in Ohio.

The auto industry has been trying to localize more production amid President Donald Trump's tariffs and inflated logistics costs that have cut into profits.

Nelson told CNBC after the event that tariffs are affecting the company's business but the new plant is more about Honda's ambitions to grow in the U.S.

"Tariffs are an impact to us, even with the high localization rate that we have," he said. "But the real impact is that we have customers out there wanting Honda product and we can't give it to them. That's the real area."

Nelson said Honda did not work with the Trump administration ahead of Monday's announcement.

Honda's plans for a new plant come roughly two weeks after Volkswagen-owned luxury brand Audi announced it would invest more than $4 billion in a new plant at a rally with President Donald Trump, which Audi CEO Gernot Dollner faced criticism for attending.

Honda's new plant is the company's first new mass production U.S. vehicle assembly plant since it opened a facility in Indiana in 2008. It also opened a $70 million performance vehicle facility in 2016 and a multibillion-dollar joint venture battery plant earlier this year, both in Ohio.

The announcement comes after the automaker in May indefinitely suspended an $11 billion investment plan to produce all-electric vehicles and batteries in Canada. It also canceled three planned EVs for the U.S. market.

American Honda Motor Co. executive Jennifer Thomas recently emphasized the Japanese automaker's commitment to the U.S. and need for additional capacity.

"In order to be competitive in this market, you're going to need to build products here," Thomas, senior vice president of corporate affairs, said Oct. 27 in Detroit. "We are focused on trying to maximize the existing capacity that we have, but we are at capacity, and so we're exploring all options."

Thomas also warned the automotive industry faces a "catastrophic" scenario if the U.S. and Canada cannot reach a new trade deal by the end of this year, when Trump has threatened to double tariffs on Canadian vehicles and parts from 25% to 50% on Jan. 1 if the two countries don't reach a deal.

Honda said it has built more than 33 million vehicles and more than 39 million engines in the U.S. since it started auto production in the U.S. in 1982.

The Japanese automaker currently has more than 30,000 employees in the U.S.

#516. Goldman CEO Solomon says AI financing remains strong despite market jitters

Goldman Sachs CEO David Solomon told CNBC on Monday that financing demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure remains strong despite recent market jitters as investors reassess the risks involving the massive build-out.

Solomon said that his firm has been "very active" in AI capital markets deals over recent weeks, a setup that he expects to continue.

"There's still very strong demand for investments in the AI infrastructure build and the technology around the growth of AI," Solomon told CNBC's Leslie Picker.

Changes in the political environment are among factors that will "create recalibrations and ups and downs, and we're seeing some of that," Solomon said. "But I still deeply believe in the long-term trajectory of the power of AI in our economy to drive productivity."

AI-related stocks, including chipmakers and neocloud providers, have tumbled in recent weeks as investors reassess whether the hundreds of billions of dollars being poured into AI infrastructure will generate good returns.

The selloff, which began before the U.S. midterm elections, intensified after Democrats scored sweeping victories on Tuesday, raising concerns that political opposition could slow the industry's expansion.

The shifting sentiment comes at a key moment for AI model lab Anthropic, which is preparing for a potential IPO that could become one of the biggest tests yet of public-market appetite for AI companies. Goldman is among the banks leading that IPO process.

"We'll see, obviously, if this IPO moves forward, how it all plays out," Solomon said, declining to comment in detail about the IPO. "I would say that the market environment is still pretty constructive."

Solomon also pushed back against political resistance to data center construction, arguing that the infrastructure is essential to the U.S. economy and that it can be built responsibly.

"We certainly don't want this infrastructure going to places outside the U.S., which is what will naturally happen because the demand for compute is there," Solomon said.

#511. Boeing to cut 5% of commercial airplane workers as production increase takes longer than expected

Boeing said Monday that it plans to cut 5% of its commercial airplane workforce as its production ramp-up is moving at a slower pace than the company expected.

The cuts will be through a combination of attrition and layoffs, Stephanie Pope, head of the commercial aircraft unit, said in a memo to staff.

Pope reiterated that the company is still working to stabilize production rates of its bestselling 737 Max planes, which are made outside of Seattle.

CEO Kelly Ortberg told investors last month that the company won't significantly increase production of the Max aircraft until the second half of next year. He said the company's wing production has been slower than expected.

"Adjusting our production schedules and reducing aircraft output puts us in an overstaffed position," Pope said in her note Monday. "Unfortunately, that means we need to align our staffing levels to our revised business plan, resulting in a reduction of approximately 5% through a combination of attrition and involuntary layoffs, with notifications beginning in December."

Boeing is targeting a production rate of 52 planes a month in the second half of 2027. Ortberg said the company has been trying to stabilize output at a rate of about 47 of its 737 Max planes a month.

#504. Outlook Went Down? Microsoft May Owe You Money

Rest assured that while Outlook, Teams, and other Microsoft services were flatlining this week, the company’s billing operations were still firing on all cylinders. Copilot can’t run on air alone, after all. It needs cash.

The silver lining is that, in the light of our global recovery from one of the most debilitating tech outages in recent memory, Microsoft now owes you compensation. And depending on the size of your operation and the degree to which you rely on Microsoft products, the payout could be substantial.

If you have a business relationship with Microsoft—a subscription to a Microsoft 365 Business plan, email hosted on Microsoft Exchange, or perhaps a Teams Premium tier—that relationship is governed by reams of documentation, among them being a service level agreement.

Service level agreements, or SLAs, are old hat to large enterprises, who use them to hold service providers like web hosts and email services accountable, ensuring they stay online and operational—and they are often in the realm of demanding 99.99 percent reliability or higher, as is the default promise with Microsoft Azure. If a provider fails to hit the promised availability level, the customer is due a refund (or credit toward next month’s bill, at least).

But SLAs don’t apply only to the Fortune 500. If you have a business account with Microsoft, no matter how small it is, your service is also governed by these promises. And now, Microsoft owes you money.

“Business account” is the key phrase in the above, mind you. If you have a Microsoft 365 Family plan or any other individual or personal plan, you are probably out of luck. Outlook could go offline for a month and you wouldn’t be entitled to anything aside from a series of pointed Facebook posts.

The consumer Microsoft Services Agreement states that individual users can get refunds or credits at Microsoft’s “sole discretion,” but it doesn’t even mention SLAs and promises nothing in the way of uptime. In other words, if your email system wasn’t working for you on November 12, you can ask for money back, but your odds of receiving anything are low.

Business users are in a much more advantageous position, though Microsoft doesn’t exactly go out of its way to guide you through the refund process. SLA refunds are not automatic. You have to request them for each incident, and the process is hardly straightforward.

To figure out what you’re owed, start by taking a look at the SLA for Microsoft’s Online Services. (This is different than the above consumer Microsoft Services Agreement.) It’s more than 53,000 words long, and quite repetitive, but I’ll distill the key points for you here.

While many of the promised service levels are identical, start by double-checking the specific Microsoft products you use, as the SLA is broken down by product. A search for “Exchange Online” reveals everything you need to know, in surprisingly plain English.

Downtime is defined as “any period of time when users are unable to send or receive email with Outlook Web Access.”

Uptime Percentage is calculated by a simple formula: Subtract the minutes of downtime from your total User Minutes, then divide the result by your total User Minutes. Take that result and multiply it by 100.

“Downtime is measured in user-minutes; that is, for each Applicable Period, Downtime is the sum of the length (in minutes) of each Incident that occurs during that Applicable Period multiplied by the number of users impacted by that Incident.”

User minutes is the total number of minutes in the billing cycle; typically 30 days.

If the calculated uptime percentage falls below 99.9 percent, you are entitled to credit (not a cash refund) under the following terms:

If we assume the outage lasted about 18 hours for you, that equates to a 2.5 percent downtime percentage or a 97.5 percent uptime percentage for a 30-day billing cycle. And that puts you squarely in 50 percent service credit territory.

While Microsoft is transparent about what you are owed, it has less to say about what you have to do to receive it.

The SLA simply states that “in order for Microsoft to consider a claim, you must submit the claim to customer support at Microsoft Corporation including all information necessary for Microsoft to validate the claim.” You are also directed to supply the following:

(i) a detailed description of the Incident; (ii) information regarding the time and duration of downtime; (iii) affected resource names; (iv) the number and location(s) of affected users; and (v) a description of errors that occurred during the incident.

That’s a lot of information, but Microsoft likely does not need much in the way of (i) , (ii), or (v) to validate this global incident. You will need to supply the name of your “affected resource” and how many users were impacted. For the sake of completeness, include in your message the codes for outages for Exchange (EX1550543), Outlook (MO1550821), and Teams (TM1550488) as relevant to your business.

I also did the math on what my various Microsoft subscriptions cost. I only have two, Exchange Online and Microsoft 365 Business Standard, both of which were impacted by the outage. Total price of my last bill: $24.88. Thus, total credit due: $12.44. That may barely be beer money for me, but a company of 100 people will likely be owed over $1,000. If you don’t have these numbers handy, you’ll find them under Billing > Bills & Payments in the Microsoft 365 Admin Center.

With that information collected, then what? Microsoft simply directs you to file a claim with customer service, so that’s exactly what I did. In the same Admin Center, the method to file an email service request is a little tricky. It’s not in the left-hand navigation but rather can be found on the bottom right of the screen. Click the headset icon, which expands to reveal “Help & support.” This will default you to Microsoft’s AI chatbot. Within this page, click the headset icon at the top of the screen, which will take you to a “Contact support” page. Choose Email from this screen, and fill in the form with the information you’ve collected.

When reached for comment, Microsoft said that for the fastest service, customers should file their request with this exact title in their service request:

“SLA Credit Service Incidents: EX1550543/MO1550821”

That title includes the codes for the Exchange and Outlook outages.

Your submission should garner a quick response that includes a case-number tracking ID. I filed for compensation on Friday and haven’t gotten any response yet other than a confirmation of receipt. But considering that 12 principle-filled dollars are on the line, you can bet I’ll be tracking it closely.

As a final note: Be aware that claims must be submitted by the end of the month following the outage. So you have until December 31, 2026, to file your claim for the November 12 incident.

When I reached out to Microsoft for comment on this story, a PR rep said she would “let me know if the company has anything to share,” while offering the same statement you have probably seen too many times already: “We are deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners. We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously.”

Now we’ll see how seriously it takes the refund process.

Update, Saturday, November 14, at 2 pm: This story was updated to include the additional comment from Microsoft about how customers should title their credit request emails.

#501. PepsiCo to cut about 1,000 jobs as North American business struggles

PepsiCo is cutting about 1,000 jobs across North America as the company tries to turn around lagging sales in its home market.

"We have a clear ambition for PepsiCo North America: to build a stronger, more future-fit business that delivers industry-leading growth," Ram Krishnan, CEO of PepsiCo North America, wrote in a Monday letter to employees obtained by CNBC. "To do that, we need to be faster, more connected, and more capable, while continuing to invest in growth — behind our people, capabilities and brands."

Over the next week, Pepsi plans to cut jobs as it says it aims to streamline its operations and better connect teams across the company's North American business. PepsiCo employs about 306,000 people worldwide, including roughly 125,000 in the United States, as of Dec. 27, according to a company filing.

The Wall Street Journal first reported the coming layoffs.

In October, PepsiCo cut its full-year earnings outlook, citing the struggles in its home market. During the third quarter, its North American beverage unit saw volume shrink 2%, while its North American food division reported flat volume.

While the company has tried to boost sales by cutting prices and investing in marketing, the turnaround is taking longer than expected, Chief Financial Officer Steve Schmitt said on the company's earnings conference call.

Shares of Pepsi rose slightly in afternoon trading. The stock is down nearly 1% this year, lagging behind the S&P 500's gains of nearly 11% over the same period.
