#460. Rivian CEO says new R2 SUV, autonomy make 2027 an ‘inflection year’ as EV maker marks five years since IPO

It's been five years since Rivian Automotive celebrated its initial public offering with CEO and founder RJ Scaringe ringing the Nasdaq opening bell from the company's factory in Normal, Illinois, while the company's sleek vehicles sat outside the stock exchange in Times Square.

It was a euphoric time for all-electric vehicle companies that has since diminished. And much like the EV hype, Rivian's stock price is down more than 80% since the Amazon-backed company debuted on the Nasdaq at $78 per share on Nov. 10, 2021. That includes a nearly 30% decline this year.

A host of internal and external factors have contributed to Rivian's stock decline, but the company continues to be the most promising EV maker to emerge during the early 2020s, as most of its peer group to go public during that time no longer exists.

Rivian founder and CEO RJ Scaringe acknowledges that not everything has gone to plan amid supply chain shortages, geopolitical tensions and slower-than-expected consumer EV adoption, but he said he believes the next year will prove to be a catalyst for the company and investors.

"2027 is really an inflection year for us," Scaringe told CNBC during an exclusive interview. "I'm excited to start to really show what we believe so fully, the potential of the business."

Scaringe's bullishness comes as Rivian ramps up production of its midsize R2 SUV — meant to expand the brand to the mainstream market — and as the company continues to invest in its next generation of vehicle software and autonomy.

Scaringe said the R2, with an entry-level model at about $45,000, remains the core business to be able to showcase its newest technologies. Rivian delivered a record 19,248 vehicles in the third quarter of 2026, up 46% year over year, and said R2 was already its best-selling vehicle by volume, outselling both its R1 flagship products and its commercial van.

"That's going to define the business. The path to profitability, the path to very significant growth in revenue is all built around R2, and then some of the variants that come following R2," Scaringe said.

For the most recent period, Rivian said the automotive cost of goods sold per vehicle improved by more than $12,000 quarter over quarter, helped by a higher R2 mix and better fixed-cost leverage.

Rivian also is in the midst of building a new multibillion-dollar Georgia factory that Scaringe has said is necessary to get the company to the volumes it needs to achieve net profitability.

Its sole factory in Normal, Illinois, is capable of producing 215,000 vehicles annually. That includes up to 155,000 R2s alongside R1 models and electric vans. Rivian said the Georgia plant will be capable of producing up to 300,000 units annually once the initial phase construction is completed in late 2028.

Of course, all of that means additional capital spending and pressure on the company to improve profits.

"We've made the decision to simultaneously work towards profitability … and deeply invest in our technology, and in particular in autonomy," Scaringe said.

Rivian's operating performance is improving, but several Wall Street analysts expect the company's investment requirements to rise significantly in 2027.

Rivian generated $176 million of consolidated gross profit in the third quarter, but its automotive business posted an $80 million gross loss, while its software and services unit generated $256 million of gross profit at a 42% margin.

Wall Street analysts broadly agree Rivian's R2 launch is showing encouraging early demand and lowering manufacturing costs, but they're concerned about whether that can offset rising autonomy spending, the Georgia factory buildout and continued cash burn.

"Funding has long been a key debate for Rivian investors," Deutsche Bank analyst Edison Yu said in an Oct. 30 investor note after the company reported quarterly results that exceeded Wall Street's expectations. "The notion of a multi-year (even if credible) path to cash flow breakeven will be less attractive to investors."

Rivian said during its late October earnings call that its total available future liquidity is expected to be about $13 billion when factoring in available credit and money from partnerships with Volkswagen and Uber as well as a $4.5 billion U.S. Department of Energy loan for the Georgia plant.

Rivian said its cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments balance was $5.5 billion to end the third quarter.

The company may still need to raise more capital. Scaringe said Rivian plans to be "opportunistic" about any additional capital raises, which have previously included equity deals with Volkswagen and Uber.

"As we think about the environment looking out, we want to continue to position ourselves to be in a place to invest in the technology. And so that means we'll be opportunistic as it pertains to capital. But I want to be clear: We're like very focused on getting the profitability," Scaringe said.

The company has significantly upgraded the software capabilities and hardware on its vehicles to prepare them for autonomy technologies, or physical artificial intelligence, as Scaringe calls it.

The next step in that is releasing "point-to-point" driving capabilities, in which the vehicles can drive on their own from destination to destination with the driver acting as a monitor in case of any unexpected problems.

U.S. EV leader Tesla is the only automaker to currently offer those features on its vehicles. Scaringe said Rivian plans to not only catch up to Tesla's capabilities but exceed them as it uses multiple sensor types to allow the vehicle to "see" better, especially in inclement weather.

"That's not like the finish line. ... Think of it as like the starting point where you suddenly have a more apples to apples comparison between Rivian and Tesla," Scaringe said.

The release of point-to-point over the coming weeks and months, first to employees and then to customers, is expected to be followed by autonomous vehicles with Uber by 2028.

Scaringe said he believes such technologies will become a differentiator for the company in the coming years.

"I think it will go from a purchase consideration for maybe 10% of the buying public that's in the top five things considered in making a purchase decision to the No. 1 or No. 2 purchase consideration for the vast majority of buyers," Scaringe said.

Rivian plans to offer more details about its AI and autonomous plans during a second annual investor technology event on Dec. 9.

"We view this as a likely positive catalyst as the event could set a path for AV features driving incremental vehicle demand & higher [software] revenue in 2027, building on 2026 growth," TD Cowen analyst Itay Michaeli said in an investor note last week.

Rivian's stock is currently rated overweight with a $17.70 target price, according to analyst estimates compiled by FactSet. That would be a roughly 26% increase from its Monday closing price but still a far cry from its IPO.

#458. The 21 Best REI Holiday Sale Outdoor Deals (2026)

REI's 2026 Holiday Sale kicked off November 13 and lasts until November 23 this year. There are plenty of great deals to help you restock your backpacking, hiking, biking, camping, outdoor-loving gear quiver. REI members can use the code HOLIDAY2026 to save an extra 20 percent off one full-price item, and an extra 20 percent off one REI Outlet item. Below are the best deals on all our favorite tents, backpacks, sleeping bags, pads, cookware, outdoor apparel, and more.

There are also sales at Backcountry.com and some of our favorite cottage industry brands like Six Moon Designs, Zenbivy, and more. Not sure what you need? Check out our many outdoor gear guides.

The 650 Down Jacket is one of the best budget three-season puffer jackets you can buy, more so at this price. At 10.9 ounces, it's reasonably lightweight and has large hand pockets and some very nice internal pockets for stashing a hat or gloves. The kids' version is also on sale in a nice yellow color that's handy for spotting your child in the snow.

The Ghost Whisperer Down Jacket is my top pick for a backpacking puffer jacket. It weighs just 6.7 ounces for a men's medium (7.3 ounces for the men's large I tested), packs down to a tiny little thing (stuffing into its own pocket), and the 1,000-fill-power goose down offers one of the best warmth-to-weight ratios on the market. The men's version is technically on sale right now, but the only size available is small and XXL.

This is my favorite puffer jacket, especially in the shoulder seasons when temps dip a little lower. It features 4.5 ounces of 1,000-fill down (men’s medium), which is half the weight of the overall jacket. The internal TILT lining (short for Thermo Ionic Lining Technology) may or may not do anything; I haven't been able to devise an effective test, but even if it did nothing, the amount of 1,000-fill-power down combined with the scant weight makes this an appealing option for ultralighters. The 7D fabric (which is what keeps the weight low) does need babying, but I haven't had any issues in over a year of use.

This is great deal on a very warm down parka. It's thigh-length, which helps keep drafts up your back to a minimum, and it fits pretty well, though it's definitely boxy (what do you want from a 650-down-fill parka that's designed primarily to keep you warm?). I did find that the main two-way zipper can be fiddly, and does sometimes snag, but it's hardly a deal-breaker, especially at this price.

Six Moon Design's Lunar Solo is one of the best single-wall ultralight solo tents you can buy. It's not without its quirks (read the pitching tips), but if you want something more than a tarp, that still weighs just 26 ounces (740 grams), this one is hard to beat. Use the Code YULEHIKE to get the 25 percent off.

Nemo's Hornet tents are some of the lightest freestanding tents around. The 1-person model is my go-to for situations where I might have to pitch on rock or concrete slabs (like staying in towns on a longer bikepacking trip). It's roomy enough for you and all your gear, light enough at 2.25 pounds (without stakes), and packs down remarkably small. It's also held up well in storms, though you will want some extra guy-outs if the wind is really howling (I like Zpacks 1.2-mm dyneema cord for lightweight guylines).

This is the bikepacking version of Big Agnes' popular Copper Spur tent. The only difference, aside from color, is that the pole segments on this model are shorter (to make it easier to store on a bike). The Copper Spur line is very nearly the lightest freestanding tent around (it's just slightly heavier than the Hornet above), and if you happen to use it with trekking poles, you get what I think is the best feature, the covered awning modification for the vestibule. While this is the best deal, the 2-person model is also on sale for 20 percent off.

This is one of my favorite ultralight sleeping bags. There are lighter quilts out there, but when you need the warmth of a mummy bag on those colder nights, this is what I use. It also has the smallest pack size of any bag I've tested in this temperature range. With the included compression sack, this thing is truly tiny. The down fill is PFC-free, 850+ hydrophobic down. The zippers are on the small side, but they slide well and rarely (if ever) snag on the bag. I've slept in this bag down to 20 degrees and never been the least bit cold.

Closed-cell foam pads like the Therm-a-Rest Z-Lite Sol are a must-have. They weigh next to nothing (10 ounces for the small), fold up small enough to lash to the outside of any pack, and double as a chair, extra padding on cold nights, table, you name it. I am too old and too soft to be the sort of ultra-minimalist who gets by with just a Z-Lite, but I still have one around on almost every trip I take.

Nemo's Tensor-insulated sleeping pads have the best R-value-to-weight ratio of anything I've tested. The Tensor All-Season featured here sports an R-value of 5.4 and weighs 18.2 ounces. That alone is impressive, but what I love about the Tensor is that it's thick, comfortable, and most importantly, nearly silent. The lighter Trail model is also on sale at REI. Its R-value is only 2.8, though.

I don't usually bring a chair backpacking—I just sit on the ground. But for some reason, I do love a chair when I'm bikepacking, which is where I've used this Nemo the most. It's quite pricey, but it's very light and packs down to a tiny package. The genius part of this chair is that the cover is also a mat that keeps it from sinking into soft ground.

The thing to keep in mind when you shop REI-brand gear is the company's basic proposition: You get 90 percent of the designer item for 70 percent of the price. It's a strategy that works quite well and has generated some really great, affordable gear. This chair is a good example of that. It's not as nice as the Nemo above—it does wobble a bit, side to side, when you move—but it's still comfortable and half the price.

I sincerely hope you never have any need for one of these Sea to Summit bug head nets, but if you do, boy do you. I once did not have one and tried to go backpacking in Michigan’s UP in early May. I lasted about two hours. When I got home, I bought one of these and … still haven't ventured back to that marshy bog of a hike. But I could, and possibly even retain some of my sanity thanks to this thing.

What I said about the chair above goes for this pack as well; it's an excellent value, especially on sale. I was surprised by how comfortable this thing is, especially because, to look at it, the straps are very lightweight and there's almost no padding. It still managed to carry loads up to 10 pounds without straining my shoulders, and the side stash pockets are fabulously large—big enough for a Nalgene bottle or rain jacket. The Flash 22 is made of 70-denier recycled ripstop nylon, which is on the lighter side, but mine has held up well, even coming through some rough canyon hikes in Utah without any more than mud stains.

These two pots are my go-to cooking vessels for larger group meals. I find they're just about perfect for boiling up enough water to rehydrate four servings. I don't take the bowls and silverware into the backcountry, but they're handy for camping.

The Stash is our favorite all-in-one backcountry cook system. The heat diffuser design is what speeds up boil times (Jetboil claims 2.5 minutes, which I was only able to match indoors in still air), but the real appeal here to me is the way this kit all packs down into itself. For weekend trips, this is the simplest system I've tested when it comes to cooking for two.

These dry bags from Sea to Summit have double-stitched, tape-sealed seams that have held up well over the years. I also really like the external lash loops that make it easy to strap to anything with a tie-down anchor.

I like these dry bags for organizing things in my pack. They're not dry bags in the sense that you can submerge them and your gear will be fine, but the 30D silnylon with PCF-free DWR easily sheds a little rain. And they make finding your stuff easy. I put all my cooking gear in a red bag (red for fire), all my water-filtering stuff in a blue bag for, wait for it, water—and so on. I find the 3- and 5-liter bags the most useful, but you can get all the way up to 35L.

I've noticed that, when trying to lighten their load with a smaller, lighter-weight backpack, people then struggle to fit all their gear. The answer for sleeping bags and clothing is this compression stuff sack, which takes my favorite sleeping bag (the Sea to Summit Spark above) and smashes it down to about half the size of the included stuff sack. You can do the same thing to clothing, especially puffer jackets, but also fleece and merino to some extent, to free up more room in your pack.

REI’s Big Haul duffel is a durable, water-resistant, gear-swallowing bag for carrying, well, just about everything. It's one of our favorite ways to schlep your gear when flying. It hits the right balance of robust build materials and pockets without being too heavy to lug around all day. The strap system is cleverly customizable, and you can convert it to a backpack.

Once upon a time, in a galaxy far, far away, I traveled around Southeast Asia with an early version of this Osprey pack, living out of it for almost a year. It stood up well to the abuse of overland travel—more than once, I watched a bus driver tie it to the roof of a bus by cinching it down with a rope, pulling with all his weight right down the middle of my pack, like a cheese cutter. The Farpoint survived, and my gear inside did as well, thanks in part to the thickly padded sides, which provide considerable extra cushioning.

#456. NYT Connections hints today: Clues, answers for November 16, 2026

The NYT Connections puzzle today is not too difficult if you like the changing of the seasons.

Connections is the one of the most popular New York Times word games that's captured the public's attention. The game is all about finding the "common threads between words." And just like Wordle, Connections resets after midnight and each new set of words gets trickier and trickier—so we've served up some hints and tips to get you over the hurdle.

If you just want to be told today's puzzle, you can jump to the end of this article for today's Connections solution. But if you'd rather solve it yourself, keep reading for some clues, tips, and strategies to assist you.

The NYT's latest daily word game has become a social media hit. The Times credits associate puzzle editor Wyna Liu with helping to create the new word game and bringing it to the publications' Games section. Connections can be played on both web browsers and mobile devices and require players to group four words that share something in common.

Each puzzle features 16 words and each grouping of words is split into four categories. These sets could comprise of anything from book titles, software, country names, etc. Even though multiple words will seem like they fit together, there's only one correct answer.

If a player gets all four words in a set correct, those words are removed from the board. Guess wrong and it counts as a mistake—players get up to four mistakes until the game ends.

Players can also rearrange and shuffle the board to make spotting connections easier. Additionally, each group is color-coded with yellow being the easiest, followed by green, blue, and purple. Like Wordle, you can share the results with your friends on social media.

#454. Fossil CEO says turnaround is ahead of plan as sales return to growth

Fossil Group posted quarterly results on Tuesday that could point to early signs of a turnaround for the legacy watchmaker.

The company, which has struggled to compete with new gadgets in the years since its sales and stock price peaked in the 2010s, has been quietly undertaking a comeback strategy under CEO Franco Fogliato. The company's fiscal third-quarter earnings indicate the strategy is beginning to bear fruit.

The company said Tuesday that it returned to profitability in the third quarter, posting operating income of $25.3 million, compared with an operating loss of $21.7 million last year. It also returned to sales growth, with net sales up 0.4% to $271.2 million. The company last reported a year-over-year sales increase in the first fiscal quarter of 2022.

"We're excited about improving dramatically the profitability of the company through a very strong and disciplined management of full-price sales as well as cost management," Fogliato told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

For the quarter ended Oct. 3, Fossil reported net income of $11.7 million, or 19 cents per share, compared with a net loss of $39.9 million, or 76 cents per share, the year prior. The company also said its operating expenses fell 11.1% to $137 million. The company last posted quarterly net income in the third fiscal quarter of 2022.

With the signs of growth, the retailer also raised its full-year outlook, guiding for full-year net sales of between $980 million and $990 million, which would mark a slight decline from just over $1 billion in net sales in the prior year. That compares with a previous expectation of net sales falling between 3% and 5%. Fossil also said it's expecting positive free cash flow by the end of the year.

Fogliato said Fossil had previously not expected sales growth for another year and a half.

The stronger quarter comes after the company saw slumping sales and financial losses over the past few years. It struggled with the rise of smartwatches, mounting debt and the decline in mall foot traffic.

Fossil stock has lost the vast majority of its value since the early-to-mid 2010s, and despite a recent surge, was trading under $8 a share on Tuesday.

To rightsize the business, Fogliato, who joined Fossil in 2024, said he focused on closing stores, moving away from promotions to full-price sales, improving marketing and broadening Fossil's product range. That meant shrinking the business to a sustainable baseline before trying to grow it.

The company saw a 5.9% decrease in direct-to-consumer sales for its third quarter, which Fogliato attributed to the company's "aggressive" downsizing.

"We simplified a lot of the business doing this," Fogliato said, adding that customers responded well when the company moved away from markdowns. "People tell me they are excited about what they are seeing in the marketplace now."

Fossil added that its key growth engine for the quarter was Asia, where net sales grew 15.1%. Fogliato said much of that growth was led by the India market.

At the same time, growing internationally is only part of the watchmaker's strategy as it tries to improve its performance in its home country.

"Priority number one is to grow our home market, and we're doing that," he said. "As retail is settling into a smaller, more profitable base, ready to grow again, we're entering 2027 with plenty of opportunities."

Fogliato said one key to growth was not trying to keep up with the smartwatch trend. While trying to make gains in the smartwatch space initially hampered Fossil's performance, it's now investing in analog watches again.

Smartwatch sales came in under $1 million for the quarter, but sales for traditional watches — which make up around 86% of Fossil's sales — grew 6.4%.

"Fossil is literally having a comeback, a strong comeback, a momentum, and we can see this," Fogliato said. "And it comes not only through the numbers — it comes with the excitement we see in the stores, and we think we're just at the beginning. We have so much work to do."

#453. It’s Time to Run an Audit of All the Cloud Storage Links You’ve Shared

Cloud storage certainly has a lot going for it. For a start, if you drop your laptop into the sink, it's likely that a lot of your important work will be backed up somewhere in the cloud, as long as you’re using services such as Google Drive, OneDrive, and iCloud.

Then there's file sharing: Not too many years ago, swapping large amounts of data with other people meant dragging and dropping and running around with USB drives (and perhaps even mailing them). Now, via a couple of clicks and a link, you can share just about anything with just about anyone, anywhere in the world.

However, this comes with security risks attached. You don't want those links making their way to anyone they weren't intended for; otherwise, they’ve got access to your files and folders. They could potentially see what’s in them and maybe even make changes or delete files, depending on the permissions you’ve set.

It’s worth emphasizing that sharing files through these cloud storage services isn't inherently insecure—these shared links are actually very well protected by default. However, the longer they stick around, the bigger the risk gets. Your links could get mistakenly forwarded in an email, or one of your contacts could have their email account hacked.

With that in mind, it’s prudent to run a regular audit on the cloud-sharing links you’ve got active. It shouldn’t take long: The apps involved usually make this pretty straightforward. Once you’ve closed a link down, no one else can get to it.

If you head to the Microsoft OneDrive portal on the web, you’ll see a Shared link in the navigation pane on the left: Click this, then select By you up at the top of the file list to see everything you’ve shared with other people. Use the Modified header if you want to sort the files and folders by the oldest or most recent.

Click the three dots next to any file or folder, then Manage access to control who can get to it. If you've shared the file with specific people, you can remove some or all of them; if the file or folder is shared more widely with just a link, clicking the trash can gets rid of it. You can also open up Office files to manage access there.

It’s worth knowing that when you opt to share a file in OneDrive on the web (via the three dots and Share), if you click the cog icon on the Share panel, you can set an expiration date for the sharing—which saves you the trouble of having to tidy it up afterward.

If iCloud Drive is your cloud storage service of choice, once you’ve got the main interface up on the web, you can click Shared on the navigation pane on the left to go straight to the files and folders you’re sharing with other people. There’s no easy way to sort them, but you can use the search box up in the top right corner.

Click the three dots next to a file or folder, then Managed Shared File to make changes. The next screen lets you block access for specific contacts and change permissions (from editing to just viewing, for example). If you want to cancel the sharing altogether, click Stop sharing.

Unlike OneDrive, iCloud doesn’t give you any way of putting expiration periods on shared links, so you have to clear them up manually.

Dropbox is one of the more aggressive services when it comes to tidying up shared links, and if you use the Dropbox app you might see reminders to review the files and folders you’ve shared with others. From Dropbox on the web, click Shared in the left-hand navigation pane to see files and folders you’re currently sharing.

You can get quite a detailed breakdown here, including by Files or by Folders. Click the three dots next to any entry, then Share, to see the current sharing options: You can remove access for contacts one by one, or click the cog icon (top left) to find the Unshare button that ends all sharing completely.

Dropbox does have link expiration features built in: If you open the share settings whenever you’re sharing a file or folder, you’ll see Link expiry as one of the options. It’s an easy way to keep things tidy, provided the people you’re sharing the file or folder with get around to viewing it in time.

Google Drive isn't quite as on top of shared links as other cloud storage services. If you open up the web portal, there’s no easy-to-access list of everything you've shared. The closest thing to it is clicking the People filter and then choosing contacts to see files and folders you've shared with them specifically. If you pick Anyone with the link from the drop-down list, you’ll see everything shared with a simple link.

To change the sharing settings for a file or folder, click on the three dots, then Share. Click the cog icon on the panel that pops up, then you can remove contacts as needed and turn the visible link off.

As with iCloud, there’s no link expiration feature you can rely on here, so shared files and folders can’t be set to become unshared after a certain time has elapsed. It’s a shame Google doesn’t make it as easy as it could be.

#448. Hurdle hints and answers for November 16, 2026

If you like playing daily word games like Wordle, then Hurdle is a great game to add to your routine.

There are five rounds to the game. The first round sees you trying to guess the word, with correct, misplaced, and incorrect letters shown in each guess. If you guess the correct answer, it'll take you to the next hurdle, providing the answer to the last hurdle as your first guess. This can give you several clues or none, depending on the words. For the final hurdle, every correct answer from previous hurdles is shown, with correct and misplaced letters clearly shown.

An important note is that the number of times a letter is highlighted from previous guesses does necessarily indicate the number of times that letter appears in the final hurdle.

#446. Apple needs your help to make health AI better

Apple is launching an app to evaluate health AI, and the company needs your help to do it.

It's all a part of a new research study, conducted in collaboration with clinical AI platform OpenEvidence, called the Apple Health AI Usage Study. The goal of the study, Apple said in a newsroom post published Sunday is to "inform the responsible design of health AI experiences for Apple and the industry."

Adults aged 21 and older from the U.S. with an iPhone 15 Pro or later can apply for the study by downloading the study app. The idea is that users ask the app health-related questions, get personalized answers based on their Apple Health data (only the bits they choose to share), with Apple looking at the questions you ask and using those insights to make health AI "smarter for everyone."

In total, Apple is opening up the app to 200,000 eligible participants.

Actually using the app appears to be similar to having a personalized health AI assistant. In one example, a user can ask the app about weight gain issues and get pointers on what to do; in another, a person with persistent lower back pain can use the app to prepare for a doctor visit.

Apple points out that neither the app nor the study provide medical advice, diagnosis, or treatment, and that medical professionals should be consulted to address actual health concerns or to verify information given by the app.

“Insights from this study will help advance the broader scientific conversation and ensure that as technology evolves, we continue building trusted tools that genuinely support people wherever they are on their health journey,” said Sumbul Desai, M.D., Apple’s vice president of Health and Fitness, in a statement.

"Participants retain control over whether to share any HealthKit metrics or survey responses, and Apple has implemented security measures to protect the study data it maintains, as well as the confidentiality of participants’ information," the company said in the post.

#442. Measles is surging in the U.S. — and outbreaks can cost millions

Measles outbreaks aren't just a public health threat. They can also be a financial burden on the U.S. — and the cost could only deepen as the once-eliminated disease spreads more widely.

The U.S. is expected to lose its official measles elimination status following a review by the Pan American Health Organization this week, after cases ratcheted to recent highs this year.

More than 5,100 measles cases have been reported nationwide in 2026 as of early November, marking the country's worst outbreak in more than three decades. Eight deaths associated with the disease have been confirmed by states so far in 2026: six in Pennsylvania, one in New York and one in Wisconsin.

Based on this year's case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses.

The tool also estimates that 2025 measles outbreaks in the U.S. — which involved 2,289 confirmed cases nationwide – carried an economic cost of more than $37 million.

The costs extend beyond treating people who become sick, accounting for the resources health departments spend investigating and containing outbreaks, as well as wages lost when people have to miss work. The tool uses more recent, state-specific cost and financing data than an initial study by the researchers, which relied on historical outbreak costs.

The estimates from the tool are not a definitive accounting of what the outbreaks actually cost. But it illustrates the hefty bill tied to the spread of a disease that can be prevented through vaccination, and the potential economic consequences of falling childhood immunization rates. CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation's public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases.

"We're starting to see a whole bunch of people who decided not to get vaccinated as kids or have never gotten exposed to measles because they benefited from herd immunity, who now make it into adulthood and risk potentially getting quite seriously ill and requiring very significant medical expenses, or dying at the hospital," Bryan Patenaude, a health economist at the International Vaccine Access Center at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, who helped develop the tool, said in an interview.

Measles outbreaks can trigger a costly public health response, even when many people who are exposed never become sick.

When a case is identified, public health officials must investigate the infection, identify and notify people who may have been exposed, coordinate with hospitals and schools, and sometimes organize vaccination campaigns to prevent further spread. Those efforts can divert staff and resources from other public health priorities.

Costs could differ heavily depending on the state.

Johns Hopkins' data includes national averages alongside state-specific inputs, including inpatient hospital costs and wages from government data or trusted health policy sources. In New York, for example, the average inpatient hospital stay is roughly $4,200 a day, while the minimum wage is $16 per hour.

The tool estimates that the 48 cases reported by New York in 2025 carried an estimated $1.1 million in costs, including nearly $749,000 in public health responses, $333,000 in productivity losses and roughly $24,500 in direct medical expenses.

Meanwhile, the 803 cases in Texas, the epicenter of the largest U.S. measles outbreak in 2025, carried estimated costs of nearly $13 million, according to the tool. That includes $9 million in public health costs, $4 million in productivity losses and around $288,000 in direct medical expenses.

The tool builds on a study published by Patenaude and other researchers in August, which examined the costs of measles outbreaks in the U.S. The researchers reviewed published studies and government reports to compile historical estimates of medical expenses and public health response costs across 18 states.

The paper estimated that outbreaks carried a historical average cost of nearly $59,000 per case, just from the public health response. The researchers also estimated how costs scale with outbreak size, separating fixed up-front response expenses from the additional costs associated with each new case.

"A lot of this is incurred up front, and that's because of the human labor that's involved in contact tracing and all of the different actors that sort of need to be mobilized to deal with the fact that there's an outbreak," Patenaude told CNBC. "It's a largely labor-driven cost."

That model estimated roughly $298,000 in fixed costs and about $16,300 for each additional case. But it relied on historical data, including a particularly large 2019 outbreak in New York City, where contact-tracing labor costs were high. The researchers also excluded productivity losses from that model because most of the historical studies they reviewed did not consistently account for them.

The economic burden of measles extends beyond the costs of treating infected patients and organizing a public health response.

People who are exposed to measles may have to quarantine, even if they never develop symptoms. Parents can also be forced to miss work to care for sick or exposed children, while workers who contract the disease may lose wages during their recovery.

A 2025 study by Joey Mattingly, a pharmacist and health economist at the University of Utah, used published epidemiological and cost data to model the potential economic burden of a hypothetical 1,500-case outbreak. His analysis included direct medical expenses, public health spending and lost productivity, and examined how the estimates changed under different assumptions, including hospitalization rates.

The study estimated that the hypothetical outbreak could cost about $90 million, including roughly $26 million in productivity losses.

Those indirect costs can add up quickly because measles is highly contagious and a single infected person can expose dozens of others. Those contacts may need to quarantine or miss work while health officials determine whether they have been infected, creating costs even when they never develop the disease.

Mattingly estimated that each person who is quarantined could amount to roughly $300 in lost productivity alone.

If a single measles case exposes 40 to 50 people who then need to quarantine, that could translate into an estimated $12,000 to $15,000 in lost productivity, even if none of those contacts become sick.

That ripple effect is one reason Mattingly argues that the economic costs of outbreaks can quickly outweigh the investment required to vaccinate people against measles.

"Even if you don't get the infection, the contact costs you," he said. "And so it's just showing that there's this multiplication or exponential growth on the economic side that happens."

The financial burden can also fall unevenly across communities. While the costs of responding to an outbreak may be shared among local, state and federal agencies, Mattingly said the impact is often felt directly by local health departments.

"It's not like your health department is sitting around waiting for measles outbreaks," he said. "They're working, doing all these other things, and imagine having to just drop what you're doing because there's been a huge outbreak in the community."

Patenaude said the findings could help state and local health departments better anticipate the resources needed to contain outbreaks — particularly as measles cases continue to rise.

"It's not super expensive to treat and deal with a single case of measles compared to a single case of cancer," he said. "But if you have these outbreaks popping up and you're having to constantly divert resources that could be spent elsewhere by state and local health departments on measles, then you're going to wind up spending a lot of money very quickly."

#440. How to Run SteamOS on Your Mac

Most of my Steam collection won't run on my Mac. It's not (entirely) my fault.

I will be the first to admit that my collection is mostly old indie games, all of which work fine on Windows. Less so on the Mac. Apple stopped supporting 32-bit games back in 2019 when it released macOS Catalina. A good chunk of my Steam library has a "No" symbol next to it because of that restriction. And next year it will get even worse: macOS will drop support for running Intel Mac applications, which could leave even more games unplayable (though Apple claims that certain older games will keep working).

But there's another way. A free, open source app called FX Steam Launcher boots the ARM version of SteamOS—the operating system that powers Valve's Steam Deck gaming handheld—right on your Mac, using a combination of a virtual machine and compatibility layers. You will need a Mac with an Apple Silicon processor, as Intel Macs aren't supported, running macOS 15 or later. I set this up recently, connected a controller, and I've been playing all kinds of games that are otherwise unplayable on my MacBook Air.

It wasn't even hard to set up. To get started, download the application, mount the DMG file, and drag the app to your Applications folder. The first time you launch the application, a pop-up will offer to download and install SteamOS for you in a virtual machine.

Click Create New Disk, and you'll be asked a few questions. The most important step is the hard drive size—the default is 64 GB, which is plenty, but consider allocating more space if you plan on installing multiple large games. You can also choose the drive location, if you like, and set a password for opening SteamOS.

Click Create, and the application will download SteamOS and install it in a virtual machine. I recommend using this time to pair a game controller with your Mac in System Settings under Bluetooth. FX Steam Launcher works with most current gamepads, including Xbox, DualSense, DualShock, and Made for iPhone. I used a Nintendo Pro Controller.

Eventually, you will see the login screen, where you can enter your Steam credentials. Once you're in, you'll see your Steam library. The virtual machine operates like an actual Steam Deck, which takes some getting used to if you've never used one. It divides your library into games known to work in SteamOS and those that might work, which is helpful. A lot of my games started running immediately, with full gamepad support.

This isn't a drop-in solution for every game. SteamOS itself is quite fiddly, and you're now running it in a virtual machine. In some cases, I needed to tweak the graphics settings for games to run correctly. For Psychonauts, specifically, I had to download a user-made patch to get the controller working properly—but I'd have needed that fix for an actual Steam Deck too. When a game ran poorly, I frequently found the solution by Googling and finding actual Steam Deck owners who ran into similar problems.

There is a limit to the processing power you get out of a Mac, especially if you (like me) are using a MacBook Air. And a virtual machine, by definition, doesn't have access to all of your machine's capabilities. But the list of compatible games, compiled from other users, shows which devices have successfully played various titles. Check that page for your favorite games before you dive in.

If nothing else, this is a lot easier than my previous method for running Windows games on a Mac, which involved setting up a version of Wine that's no longer supported. I even got a few non-Steam games installed. Sideloading on SteamOS means booting into Desktop Mode, which you can find in the SteamOS sidebar under Power. You'll end up in a Linux-based desktop, complete with a web browser. You can download games, put them in the folder of your choice, and point Steam to them. I couldn't add custom art, but I got multiple games up and running this way.

Honestly, Valve should update the official Steam Mac client with similar compatibility tech so it can run Windows games natively. It would be a huge hit with Mac users.

If you're a power user, I recommend checking out the settings for FX Steam Launcher, which you can open from the menu bar or with the keyboard shortcut Command-comma. You can change the resolution for your virtual Steam Deck, configure the sound to your liking, and submit game statistics.

It's remarkable software. And, with an HDMI cable and a gamepad, you can plug your MacBook into your TV and game from your couch. That's what I plan on doing when I'm done writing this article (which I guess is right now).

Nintendo Store — Out of stock right now, but you can add it to your wishlist to get pinged the second a restock hits.

Amazon — Dropping tiny batches of consoles randomly throughout the day.

Walmart — Stock exists but only for third-party listings selling at way over market price, so make sure your in-stock email alerts are turned on.

Target — Sold out online, but periodic drops have taken place in-store.

Best Buy — Sold out online, but select stores ran midnight openings on Nov. 5 with walk-in stock.

GameStop — Online orders are off; you will have to walk into a store to find one.

Landing limited-edition Nintendo hardware takes a little prep work, but these battle-tested habits will give you a real edge over the bots:

Save your checkout details everywhere — Log in to Target, Best Buy, Walmart, and the Nintendo Store to save your shipping address and default card. Stopping to type a card number or billing zip code during a live drop is how you lose an order.

Hands off the refresh button in Best Buy's queue — When an online drop goes live, Best Buy puts shoppers into a randomized waiting line to filter out scrapers. Touching the reload button resets your spot and boots you straight to the back.

Skip the web wars and head to physical stores — Online checkout bots grab website stock in under 15 seconds. Showing up in person is easily your most reliable path to securing one without paying scalper markups.

Stores like Walmart, Amazon, and Best Buy often hold onto popular gaming hardware to use as headline deals during Black Friday and Cyber Monday week.

With that said, Black Friday restocks are usually the hardest ones to catch. You will be fighting website crashes, gift shoppers, and automated bots all at once. If you can grab a console whenever stock appears, take it. Think of Black Friday as a backup plan, not your main bet.

Console hunting sucks right now, period. But between the surprise micro-drops and physical store restocks, you still have real shots at retail price — just be ready to move the second that stock goes live.

#435. JetBlue unveils new first-class seats to bring back profits

JetBlue Airways unveiled its new domestic first-class seats Wednesday as more of the country's carriers bet on the premium travel boom to make them profitable again.

JetBlue announced it would offer a domestic first class — BlueFirst — in December 2024 and plans to start flying aircraft with the new seats and full on-board product next month. The carrier showed off the new cabin at its home hub at John F. Kennedy International Airport on Wednesday. Retrofits will continue during 2027, CEO Joanna Geraghty told CNBC late last month.

JetBlue will have 12 of the seats on its Airbus A320 fleet and 30 extra-legroom seats, down from 42 of those options previously. Because the airplanes don't have ovens, JetBlue said it is offering an array of cold food options like sesame noodles, pastrami sandwiches and tortellini salad, though they vary by route.

The rollout comes as the New York airline tries to stabilize after last posting a profit in 2019. Last month, JetBlue executives sought to calm investors after shares in the airline hit a record low following a report that bondholders had contracted restructuring advisors.

"We have no intention, no plans to file for bankruptcy. We are not engaged with any restructuring company," Geraghty told CNBC in an interview on Oct. 27.

Geraghty told CNBC's Phil LeBeau on Wednesday that JetBlue has "met with a few of the creditors over the last couple of weeks as part of ... normal investor engagement." She said the company made it clear to bondholders that JetBlue doesn't need to reorganize.

The airline's shares are up more than 40% this month, more than other passenger carriers, as of midday Wednesday, though they are still off about 11% so far this year.

JetBlue is betting that resilient travel demand, especially for roomier seats will help fuel its turnaround plan, which aims to get the carrier to an operating profit next year and $1 in adjusted earnings per share in 2028.

Airline executives have said they expect fares to stay elevated next year as they grapple with higher costs, including but not limited to fuel, such as labor and airport fees. Geraghty said Wednesday that even if fuel prices moderate next year, "I don't think fares are going to fall as much as people expect."

Airlines large and small are racing to outfit their cabins with roomier, more expensive seating options to help cover ever-higher costs, from jet fuel to labor to airport operation fees.

For example, a ticket in the new recliner first-class seats between Kennedy airport and Fort Lauderdale-Hollywood International Airport in Florida is going for $847 roundtrip in the first week of March while standard economy is $446.

On Tuesday, budget carrier Frontier Airlines unveiled its new first-class seats, which will take up the first two rows, and said it would outfit its Airbus fleet with premium seating that has extra legroom and in-seat power through row eight. The airline will take out a row of seating on its planes to make the pricier ones fit, Chief Marketing Officer Bobby Schroeter told CNBC in an interview Monday.

JetBlue last month reported that it ended the third quarter with $2.2 billion in liquidity and that its unencumbered asset base is now $7 billion, including $2.8 billion in aircraft and engines.

JetBlue had total debt of $8.4 billion as of Sept. 30, the carrier said in its earnings filing.

Geraghty told CNBC last month there is a "fundamental disconnect between the narrative in the bond market and what our financial results actually demonstrate."

When asked whether JetBlue's eye on keeping costs down includes furloughs, layoffs or buyouts, Geraghty last month said "absolutely not."

#433. Pokémon TCG 30th Celebration Ultra-Premium Collections: Live restock updates at Amazon, Walmart, and Target (Nov. 16)

The Pokémon TCG's 30th Celebration Ultra-Premium Collections have finally arrived, but getting hold of either the Espeon (Day) or Umbreon (Night) edition at a reasonable price has been proving difficult, even with new listings regularly going live across major U.S. trading card retailers.

Released on Nov. 6, these enormous anniversary boxes have become two of the most sought-after Pokémon products of the year — just as much as the 30th Celebration ETB. Along with the many early Pokemon Black Friday deals, we've found several places to buy both versions, with Walmart currently offering the lowest prices among top retailers. However, there are some important differences between the available offers and the official restocks.

We've rounded up the retailers worth checking out, the latest prices, and everything you need to know to secure one of these special collections ahead of Black Friday.

These are the main U.S. retailers to bookmark for the Espeon and Umbreon collections, including their current stock situation:

Amazon: Stock is coming and going directly from the retailer and third-party sellers, so we’ve added a go-to search results page to quickly check for any restocks (we expect more this week).

Best Buy (Day and Night): Official stock is hard to find, but third-party listings are still available for both versions. Set up deal alerts to catch any new allocations.

Target (Day): Currently sold out via official stock and third-party sellers.

TCGplayer (Day and Night): The dedicated trading card marketplace has listings for both the Espeon and Umbreon editions of 30th Celebration’s Ultra-Premium Collection at around or under market price.

Walmart (Day and Night): Both Day and Night editions are available through third-party sellers. Keep an eye out for cheaper listings.

Demand for the 30th Celebration expansion has been exceptionally strong since its September launch, and these Ultra-Premium Collections are arguably the hottest items in the entire anniversary lineup.

Unlike smaller Pokemon products such as Booster Bundles and Elite Trainer Boxes, each UPC contains a staggering 30 booster packs, including one exclusive Classic Collection pack with three etched cards celebrating the TCG's history. Add in two highly collectible promo Pokémon cards — Espeon and Umbreon — alongside special Pikachu ex promo cards and all the premium extras (playmat, card sleeves, deck box, etc.), and it’s no wonder that there hasn’t been enough supply to meet the demand.

There are two versions to choose from: the green-and-blue Day edition featuring Espeon ex, and the purple-themed Night collection starring Umbreon ex.

Both contain 29 standard 30th Celebration booster packs and one special Classic Collection pack, alongside a foil Pikachu ex promo card and a matching Espeon ex or Umbreon ex promo. You'll also get a themed playmat, 65 card sleeves, a deck box, a metal Pokémon coin, six damage-counter dice, and a code card for Pokémon TCG Live.

The accessories and promotional artwork vary between editions, but you’re effectively getting the same level of contents with either box.

With both versions in high demand, there are a few ways to make tracking down an Espeon or Umbreon Ultra-Premium Collection a little easier:

Keep checking the major retailers: Walmart, Target, Best Buy, Amazon, and GameStop are all worth revisiting throughout the week — as new stock has been constantly reappearing over the last couple of weeks. On the other hand, stock can sell out with little warning, so you’ll need to check regularly.

Set up stock alerts where available: Retailer notifications and independent stock trackers can help you spot fresh inventory without repeatedly refreshing every product page. Best Buy, for instance, even lets you set deal alerts for specific listings.

Compare prices across different sellers: Third-party sellers can be a useful way to secure either collection when official stock is unavailable. Just remember to compare their offers against current market prices (available on TCGplayer), as some listings are considerably more expensive than others.

Check whether you're getting Day or Night: Some retailers list the two versions separately, while GameStop uses combined listings with randomized selections. Make sure you're ordering the right product if you're specifically after Espeon or Umbreon.

Don't overlook in-store availability: Online stock shifts incredibly fast, but checking nearby Target, Best Buy, and GameStop locations could give you another chance to grab a box — as many don’t check physical stores, simply assuming they’ll be automatically sold out, particularly if additional inventory arrives ahead of Black Friday.

It's certainly possible that more Ultra-Premium Collections could come in stock for Black Friday, though no allocations have been confirmed for either collection.

With Black Friday arriving on Nov. 27, there's still a little over two weeks for retailers to receive more inventory and, as mentioned, there have been fleeting stock drops across the major Pokemon TCG retailers — even if most of them come via third-party sellers. Keep in mind that due to the demand for the 30th Celebration UPCs, sellers have little incentive to discount listings much below market price (around $360 per Ultra-Premium Collection).

For now, it's worth keeping the retailer pages above close at hand, so you can compare prices for any available stock and decide what’s best for your trading card budget.

Ben Williams is a freelance writer at Mashable, having joined the team in June 2025. With over 10 years experience in gaming, tech, TV, anime, and film, there’s nothing he hasn’t covered. Alongside Ben’s other work at IGN, Radio Times, Eurogamer, UNILAD Tech, and Rock Paper Shotgun, he also has bylines at sites like GamesRadar+, PCGamesN, ScreenSphere, Twinfinite, ScreenRant, GGRecon, and more.

When away from writing, he spends his downtime playing and watching games, films, and shows he loves covering — whether it’s replaying The Last of Us Part 2, catching Pokémon, rewatching Fullmetal Alchemist: Brotherhood, or bingeing Tarantino movies.

#432. I Put the Best Digital Notebooks to the Test. Here Are My Favorites (2026)

Do you take a lot of notes? Whether it's daily to-do lists, notes for class, or just jotting down thoughts, I've found the best digital notebooks to keep them organized. Sure, you could just open your Notes app, but physically writing something down helps you remember and learn more. Plus, I just love to write things down, and these digital notebooks keep me from burning through paper or losing a great idea I wrote down.

Most of these tablets use E Ink technology to give you a more paperlike experience to write your ideas and notes on. It's easy on the eyes and helps keep you distraction-free as you take your notes as a classic notebook would. As our team's resident obsessive notetaker, I've tried almost every digital notebook on the market to find the best ones worth buying. Whether you want to be able to take detailed notes and access them on your laptop, annotate a book, or have near-never-ending battery life, I've found a digital notebook for you. They're especially great for students who need to do a lot of note-taking and stay engaged without getting distracted by notifications and other apps.

If your notes need an upgrade, I highly recommend giving these a try. If you're looking for more organizational tools and e-paper gadgets, don't miss my guides to the Best Paper Planners, Best Digital Wall Calendars, and Best E-Readers That Aren't a Kindle.

Updated November 2026: The Supernote Manta is now our runner-up pick, and the Supernote Nomad is now our budget pick.

The ReMarkable Paper Pro has everything we like about the ReMarkable 2—a matte screen feels like paper, quick and responsive writing experience, and a great battery life—and adds a few cool new features like colorful pen options and a gentle built-in screen light that uses ambient light from the room. It's got an 11.8-inch screen, making it an extra inch and a half bigger than the ReMarkable 2. It's especially handy if you're taking notes or writing down ideas somewhere a little dark, like a dimmed classroom or on a late flight. Like the ReMarkable 2, it's easy to organize your notes in various ways, from different notebooks to tags and folders. It's easy to access notes off the device, too, especially if you have ReMarkable's Connect subscription ($4 per month) for unlimited cloud storage. The stylus charges on the device, too.

The Paper Pro does have its own set of accessories, including covers ($179) to protect it and a keyboard folio ($229) if you want to type with it, since it's a different size than the ReMarkable 2. It has its own set of styluses as well, the Marker and Marker Plus—the Marker Plus is a $50 upgrade, but I find the eraser to be well worth it.

The Supernote Manta is the second generation of Supernote's A5 digital notebook, and it's a great option with new upgrades like a dual slider bar, upgraded RAM, and FeelWrite 2 film covering the screen to give it a feeling similar to paper. You can also expand the storage with a microSD card, and there's a modular design under the hood so that you can easily upgrade the motherboard or put in a new battery.

The software is easy to use and has a design similar to ReMarkable’s, with plenty of templates to choose from for your notes. There’s also a cloud website and app you can use to send PDFs to the digital notebook to edit, and you can also integrate it with apps like Google Drive and Dropbox. You can also add on the app Atelier to get a digital sketchbook option (though it's only in shades of grayscale), which I found fun and easy to use for a little drawing. Supernote will also convert your handwriting into a typed document, but you'll need to select that type of document from the beginning rather than just use a tool to convert any handwriting in any file.

But where Supernote really shines is that it has, in my opinion, the best pens in the game. My favorite is the clear LAMY Safari Vista EMR ($99) pen that looks so realistic, I keep grabbing it to write in my paper planner, but it's currently out of stock. However, there's still the Heart of Metal ($83) pen that also has an impressively realistic feel to it, with a little more weight to it thanks to the metal body. Ultimately, if you're a bit of a pen and stationery nerd (and don't need a keyboard attachment, which Supernote doesn't offer), then you'll get your heart's fill with Supernote's pen lineup paired with its great digital notebook.

This is a smaller version of the Supernote Manta, measuring almost 6 inches on one side and 7.5 inches on the other. Supernote designed it with travel in mind, but I find it to be a great size for a daily digital to-do list or writing down quick notes from a meeting. It's a good size to stack on top of other notebooks or slide into a variety of bags with its smaller size.

It's too small for anything major, though it's not as small as the reMarkable Paper Pro Move, which has a slim reporter's notebook-style design. The Supernote Nomad has a more similar size and feel to an e-reader, which is great if you're not looking for something large to tote around. It's also a good price point, though once you add on the pen it'll cost a little more than the older Kindle Scribe and reMarkable Move, so you can buy those if you want to spend the least amount possible to get a device. You'll get a larger screen with the Supernote Nomad, but the reMarkable Paper Pro Move has a color screen if you'd rather prioritize that with your budget. Supernote also designed this digital notebook with repairability in mind like the Manta, and has guides on its website to allow you to replace the battery, motherboard, and microSD card.

Kobo makes some of our favorite e-readers, and the Kobo Libra Colour takes its e-reader software and not only adds digital notebooks, but color annotating, too. You can write in the margins and underline your favorite quotes using eight different colors (plus black and gray, giving you 10 choices total), and you can hold down the button on the Stylus 2 ($70) to enter highlight mode. You can only highlight books in yellow, but there are four highlighter colors in notebook mode. The Stylus 2 also has an eraser on the back that works as an object eraser, so one tap to the highlighted area or on-page doodle and the whole thing will vanish from your book's page.

The Libra also has a separate notebook section, where you can create different digital notebook pages. You can choose from a variety of layouts for each page (click the three dots and choose “change page background”) in a notebook, plus three different pen styles, a highlighter, and a paintbrush to use with the eight colors and two neutrals in the Libra's digital palette. It is a small size, so good for taking quick notes but not ideal for, say, writing down everything from an intense lecture. My handwriting also didn't appear as clearly as it did on ReMarkable and Scribe devices. But if you're more of a quick-list or idea kind of person and mainly want to annotate your e-books with color, the Kobo Libra Colour will be perfect for you. It recently rose in price, from $230 to $260, so I'd wait to buy it on sale rather than pay the new MSRP.

★ Larger, but no color: We previously recommended the Kobo Elipsa 2E ($400) for readers, and while it's still a great device, I think it's just a little too large for frequent readers (I much prefer the smaller Libra to bring books with me on the go), and missing out on color is a huge bummer. But I prefer both Kobos to the Kindle Scribe for annotating while I read, and the Elipsa and Scribe are similar sizes.

Is the second-generation Amazon Kindle Scribe the best digital notebook? No. It's a bit of a hybrid. I'd describe it as an extra-large Kindle that happens to have some digital notebook abilities. But time and time again, I find myself reaching for my Kindle over the other digital notebooks in my house. I'm a big reader, and a Kindle user at that, so having one device that already has all my books that can also serve as my personal doodle pad has been really convenient. It also helps that it has the best battery life of up to 12 weeks, so I almost never need to charge it before using it.

ReMarkable and Supernote have more templates and compatibility, and Kobo's devices let you actually write notes on the book. Kindle only lets you write on Post-It-like pop-ups, called Active Canvas, and an expanded sidebar margin that can pop up and take up a third of the page for you to write on. If you're looking to actually annotate a book, the Kobo Libra (more on that below) is a better choice. Both the Kobo Elipsa and Libra give you more freedom to actually write on book pages, and the Libra gives you the option of color.

The Scribe does now come with new generative AI features (Summarization and Refined Writing), but they’re subtle. You're able to sync the notebooks on your Scribe to the Kindle app, but these don't sync as seamlessly as the ReMarkable app does. And yet, since I'm already a Kindle user, I find myself always grabbing this notebook to read my latest library book or jot down ideas on a notepad. It's serving me well for my minimalist needs—I use my notebook for doodling ideas and writing lists rather than taking notes for class or uploading PDFs to edit—but our recommendations above are more powerful with better accessories.

If you like the price of this model but want more AI features (a pass for me, personally) and the ability to connect to Google Drive and Microsoft OneDrive, and don't mind losing out on the front light, check out my notes below on the Kindle Scribe Without Front Light ($430). You can also spend a little more on the third-gen Kindle Scribe ($500) to get a front light, or even more on the Kindle Scribe Colorsoft ($630) to get a color screen. I still prefer the older second-gen Kindle Scribe, but you can read all about the newer models below.

What if you could have 80 percent of what's great about an E Ink screen and still watch videos and use your tablet like, well, a tablet? That's the premise behind TCL's Note A1 Nxtpaper, which wants to be the best of both worlds. The screen is not E Ink, and the writing experience is correspondingly not E Ink–good here, but it's good enough for many, and the fact that when I'm done taking notes I can go stream a video makes the Note A1 more versatile than most tablets on this page. The Note A1 includes a very nice reversible pen (you can erase with the top) and a keyboard. The unnecessarily complex keyboard case requires a deep understanding of origami to use properly, but it protects the device, and the keyboard is nice to type on (it's included in the price).

The custom user interface that TCL has built makes this device decidedly note-focused. The home screen consists of a small to-do list, a clipboard shelf, and then a list of all your notes. Click on a note with the pen, and it will open in edit mode with a configuration toolbar shown across the top. Here you can set up pen type, color, line thickness, and all the other tools you'd want to use to write or draw. I found the editing interface just about perfect in the sense that it balances keeping it simple but still gives you access to the controls you actually need. There are, however, some quirks here. For example, you can't easily switch from reading a note to editing. For the most part, though, the UI works well for the intended purpose (primarily, taking notes). There are some nice AI-driven features here to help with note-taking, including automated meeting summaries, audio-to-text transcription, and handwriting-to-text conversion (also, equations can be converted from handwritten to typed). Underneath its custom UI, this is an Android tablet with the Google Play Store at your disposal, so you can run all the usual apps. —Scott Gilbertson

Neo Smartpen M1+ for $129: If you don't want an entire tablet to write your digitized notes on, you can get a smart pen to use on regular paper that will log your notes into its companion app. The Neo Smartpen M1+ was the thinnest and lightest smart pen former WIRED reviewer Medea Giordiano tried, and she easily used it with notebooks and planners alike. You'll pair it with the Neo Studio app, which has a notebook system that's easy to navigate and organize your notes.

Neo Smartpen Dimo for $59: This is the cheapest smart pen option and uses replaceable batteries. It isn't compatible with Google Calendar, iCal, or Outlook like our other picks.

Boox Note Air4 C for $470: If you're looking for something a little more powerful than just taking notes and annotating, but still want the E Ink screen experience, then a Boox device is what you need. The Boox Note Air4 C has an e-paper screen and is primarily described as an “e-paper notebook,” but runs on Android 13 and has access to the Google Play Store, giving it more of a tablet feel that happens to have a colorful e-paper screen instead of your usual brightly lit iPad or Android tablet. You're able to easily access the web (called NeoBrowser) right from the Boox homepage, which usually takes several steps and is incredibly slow on a true digital notebook. You'll also access apps that look like a tablet, including an image gallery, calendar, music player, and tools like a clock, recorder, and calculator. It was a pick in this guide, but it's now out of stock for US warehouses (you can still order from the Hong Kong warehouse, but you might have to pay extra tariffs and fees), and there's a new version out, the Note Air5 ($530).

The 3rd Gen Kindle Scribe (left) and the Kindle Scribe Colorsoft.

Kindle Scribe Colorsoft for $630 and Kindle Scribe (3rd Gen) for $500 and Kindle Scribe Without Front Light for $430: The new versions of the Kindle Scribe are still solid all-around devices that have a good mix of e-reader and digital notebook features, but I still prefer the older model's price point. While the new Scribe Colorsoft is a nice option if you want color, I think these are too expensive for what they are. The price comes from all the AI features packed into the device, plus connectivity to services like Google and Microsoft, but it still isn't as powerful as a ReMarkable or Supernote if you want better tools. My picks above are a better price for similar features, though the Scribe does have the most impressive battery life of any digital notebook device I've tried (and that's still true for the older model that I recommend above, too, so you don't need the newest model for the best battery life).

Moleskine Smart Writing Set for $225: Moleskine is already known for its notebooks, and the brand has a smart version now, too. Our team previously tested a version in 2018 that only cost $149, but the current version that's $224 is next on my list to test. The set comes with a notebook and pen that will pair with the Moleskine Notes app to show you your notes.

Montblanc Digital Paper for $950: Yes, that Montblanc, the one with the famous pens, now has a digital notebook of its own. It's no surprise that I'd call its digital pen one of the best. It's got a super-lightweight feel while sporting three different buttons, two of which you can customize. (There's no eraser, but I'll give it up for that end of the pen to instead be a home button). I really like this device overall, but it's pricey for what you get—aside from the customizable pen buttons, there's not much to differentiate it from my top picks.

ReMarkable Paper Pro Move for $449: The ReMarkable Paper Pro Move is similar in size to a reporter's notebook, which turns out to be a pretty great size for daily task and to-do lists. There's room for a multilevel to-do list and brainstorming, and it's a nice, small size with just a 7.3-inch screen. There are other small options on the market, but this is the only one designed like a long notebook rather than a square, e-reader-like design. I like this size to layer on top of a paper planner or slip into a bag. It can do everything the larger Paper Pro can, including packing a color screen, and honestly feels like you sliced off a bottom third of the device and kept using it.

ReMarkable Paper Pure for $399: This is another good budget option if you want something larger than the Supernote Nomad. It has the same price as the second-generation Kindle Scribe I adore, but with more powerful digital notebook tools instead of a focus on reading. If you're looking for a great digital notebook without spending the better part of a grand, ReMarkable's new Paper Pure is its new basic digital notebook with a black-and-white screen, a lightweight feel, and a great writing experience that is one of the closest things you'll get to writing on actual paper. It doesn't have a front light, though, so you need to use it in lit rooms only.

ReMarkable 2 for $419 (Refurbished): This is the older ReMarkable digital notebook from 2020, and it's still a great device with a great range of accessories—while they're still available, that is. This device is only available refurbished or secondhand after getting essentially replaced earlier this year with the ReMarkable Paper Pure.

Rocketbook Fusion Plus for $30: If you really prefer the paper experience and don't want something you need to charge, then Rocketbook is for you. Rocketbook makes several reusable notebooks, planners, and accessories like index cards and sticky notes. I like the Rocketbook Fusion Plus since it comes with a ton of different template styles, from your classic monthly and weekly pages to project management layouts and meeting notes, making it feel closer to the variety a digital notebook can offer you. Take notes with an erasable Pilot Frixion Pen (one comes with whichever item you buy), scan photos of the pages into the Rocketbook app, and erase the whole thing with the damp microfiber cloth (also included). The app is designed to keep everything organized and easily send things off to Google Drive, Slack, Trello, OneNote, and a handful of other options.

Do You Need a Digital Note-Taking Device?

The short answer is no. These devices are expensive, usually costing several hundred dollars when a simple notebook and pen costs a few bucks. But if you like the physical act of writing but need to have digital copies, they're worth considering.

E Ink notebooks are easier on the eyes and feel closer to paper than a glass-screened tablet. But in some cases, you might spend less getting an iPad and an Apple Pencil (and a keyboard if you also want to type sometimes). You can also do more on them thanks to the thousands of apps in the App Store. An iPad is pretty intuitive if you're familiar with the Apple ecosystem (though both ReMarkable and Supernote's ecosystems are also very intuitive). Past WIRED reviewers liked using an iPad with the GoodNotes and Notability apps; check out our guide to the Best iPads for more advice on which to buy.

I also have an iPad, and I'll say the upside of a digital notebook is that the battery tends to last longer, especially if you're the type to use it for a day or two and then forget about it for a few days. My trusty Kindle Scribe usually manages to have plenty of battery available for whenever I feel the note-taking itch, even if it's been a week or two since I last touched it.

Just because these devices look like paper doesn't mean they're as cheap to make as a sheet of paper. Like any tablet or smartphone, these devices are packed with features, a special ecosystem, and the E Ink technology is no small feat to perfect, along with a sensation similar to writing on paper. It's an impressive piece of technology.

Prices haven't gotten cheaper for this category in the wake of tariffs' causing technology prices to rise. ReMarkable recently raised the price of our top pick, the ReMarkable Paper Pro. Another favorite of ours from Supernote has gone up a little less than $50, and Kobo also slightly raised its prices, though the $10 increase is pretty minor compared to other increases we’ve seen. We'll continue to monitor prices and flag if more price changes are to come.

Struggling to get your hands on the Zelda edition of the Switch 2? How about grabbing a discounted Switch 2 instead, weeks ahead of Black Friday?

Yes, you read that right. Nintendo's console rarely goes on sale — and even less so since its $50 price hike in September — yet Woot is offering a brand-new Nintendo Switch 2 for $469. The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

#426. American Airlines brings fresh food back to NY-Chicago first class for first time since 2019

American Airlines said Wednesday it's bringing small, fresh "bites" to first class on flights between its hubs in New York and Chicago, the carrier's latest effort to win over higher-spending customers.

The changes start this month. Customers will be able to pick between two options, which include prosciutto and melon or apple cinnamon chia pudding for breakfast and a Thai noodle salad or mezze plate for lunch and dinner, American said Wednesday.

American last offered fresh food for first class on these routes in 2019, a spokeswoman said. Currently, customers in first class on these flights get a smaller, packaged snack. Airlines largely have limited food options for shorter flights, even in first class.

American has fallen behind Delta Air Lines and United Airlines in profits and investments in premium options. The airline has been working to step up those premium offerings in recent years with new cabins, lounges and plans for seatback screens on its narrow-body fleets.
