#425. Beware the New AI-Fueled Ransomware Boom

With so much going on all the time, you may not have thought about ransomware and data extortion attacks in a minute, even though they’ve been among the the biggest cybersecurity threats to institutions—and, by extension, people—over the past decade. But in the new era of AI-powered and automated cyberattacks, the danger posed by ransomware is only increasing as cybercriminals profit by combining established methods with new techniques made possible or more accessible by AI.

It is difficult to determine exact numbers of ransomware attacks, given that victims don’t always disclose incidents and attackers wildly inflate their claims. But researchers who specialize in tracking and attempting to de-duplicate the field agree that claimed incidents have spiked over the last few months. Some put the number of alleged ransomware attacks on leak sites—where groups post alleged victims—at nearly 3,000 attacks over the summer. This would mean a 20 to 40 percent increase since the spring by some estimates. And, crucially, researchers emphasize that while volume always fluctuates somewhat, ransomware attacks overall have been consistently and concerningly elevated in recent years.

“What we see is most of the recent increase was fueled by international attacks; the United States was a little more steady, but still high, still a lot of attacks,” says Cynthia Kaiser, former FBI cyber deputy director who now leads research for the anti-ransomware platform Halcyon. “Internationally we’re seeing a lot more attacks. That to me is the really new part, and I do think it is in part fueled by AI.”

Fully AI-powered ransomware attacks have yet to emerge, perhaps because the criminal business model already works so effectively. But cybercriminals do appear to be using LLMs for some of the more mundane aspects of their attacks.

“We are seeing AI relied on more heavily in certain aspects of the ransomware attack chain, such as creating better phishing emails and in the negotiation process, but the ‘successful’ ransomware groups are still largely human operated,” says Allan Liska, a longtime ransomware researcher and threat intelligence analyst at Recorded Future. “But the fact that these groups are experimenting heavily with AI means that this could change going forward, and change quickly.”

Kaiser notes that broader trends in scamming are always relevant to ransomware, because attackers often use the same types of phishing and social engineering techniques to gain initial access to targeted systems. In Japan, for example, successful scamming has spiked drastically in recent months as AI enables foreign attackers to generate consistently convincing content in Japanese for the first time.

A few ransomware groups—namely, the Gentlemen, Qilin, and Akira—have emerged as some of the most prominent and stable operations in recent months. However, in their shadow is a range of smaller groups, emerging actors, and fragmentation of the ransomware ecosystem. In recent weeks, multiple new ransomware groups have appeared and quickly claimed to have attacked dozens of businesses. Halycon’s analysis says around 111 ransomware groups made claims of attacks between July and September this year.

One new group calling itself Global Secret Group claimed attacks on 48 victims, with more than half of these being listed on the same day, according to security firm ReliaQuest’s analysis. Another, called Storm, listed 71 victims in just 20 days. Not all of the attacks may be legitimate, but they still cause uncertainty for businesses.

“That growth has all happened because, in my eyes, this acceleration that AI has provided to these threat actors, essentially allowing for them to go out and start their own groups,” says Brandon Tirado, the director of threat research at ReliaQuest.

Instead of being beholden to traditional “ransomware-as-a-service” providers, all of the researchers Kernel Panic spoke to say they have seen more lone-wolf operators branching out. Tirado says his team recently managed to identify who is behind one new group and found that it is being run by an inexperienced teenager who has made more than $800,000 in under two weeks. He declined to name the teen publicly.

Halcyon’s Kaiser agrees that more actors are using AI to attempt to strike out on their own with expanded capabilities to trick targets, exploit vulnerabilities, check their code, and even compromise other groups’ remote-access tools to gain redundant access to a victim in case one avenue gets blocked. But she adds that it’s still not clear how successful these upstarts will be long-term. She suspects that many of them may surge initially and then burn out, because they don’t have the institutional knowledge and expanded resources of an established group.

While established attackers typically conduct “double extortion” attacks in which they deploy traditional ransomware that encrypts a victim’s systems while also exfiltrating a target’s data and then threatening to publish it online, those operating independently may be more likely to focus solely on data extortion to simplify their operations.

“The number of attacks that Recorded Future has seen this year already dwarfs the total number for 2025,” Liska says, “but the number of attacks involving encryption may actually decrease year over year.”

#421. Moon phase today: What the Moon will look like on November 16, 2026

We're almost at the First Quarter Moon, which is when we'll see half of the lunar surface illuminated. From here, the Moon will continue to grow until we reach the Full Moon again.

As of Monday, Nov. 16, the NASA's Daily Moon Guide tracker tells us the Moon is in the Waxing Crescent phase, with 39% of its surface visible.

If you don't have any visual aids, you can still see the Mares Tranquillitatis, Fecunditatis, and Serenitatis. With binoculars or a telescope, you'll also see the Endymion Crater, Posidonius Crater, and the Mare Nectaris. And for keen Moon gazers with a telescope, you'll get to see all this plus the Apollo 16 and 17 landing spots, as well as the Rima Ariadaeus.

The next Full Moon will take place on November 24.

The Moon takes approximately 29.5 days to complete one full cycle. As it orbits Earth, the amount of sunlight reflecting off its surface that we can see changes, creating the different phases. This takes us from the New Moon to the Full Moon and back again, before the cycle begins once more, according to NASA.

New Moon - The Moon is between Earth and the sun, so the side we see is dark (in other words, it's invisible to the eye).

Waxing Crescent - A small sliver of light appears on the right side (Northern Hemisphere).

First Quarter - Half of the Moon is lit on the right side. It looks like a half-Moon.

Waxing Gibbous - More than half is lit up, but it's not quite full yet.

Full Moon - The whole face of the Moon is illuminated and fully visible.

Waning Gibbous - The Moon starts losing light on the right side. (Northern Hemisphere)

Third Quarter (or Last Quarter) - Another half-Moon, but now the left side is lit.

Waning Crescent - A thin sliver of light remains on the left side before going dark again.

#419. New Species of Giant Fungus Discovered in China Grows up to 20 Inches Tall

Sometimes new species evade detection because they’re tiny and thus easy to miss—not this giant fungus. Shaped like a hand reaching out of the ground, this new species of fungus was discovered deep in the forest in China’s Yunnan Province. A research team from the Kunming Institute of Botany, part of the Chinese Academy of Sciences, reported it as a new species they named Dianjunus rex.

The name comes from the region’s ancient Dian Kingdom and the Chinese pronunciation of the word fungus. D. rex is a type of Ascomycete, the largest phylum of fungi, with over 64,000 known species, and among them, this new fungus produces the largest masses of mushroom recorded. Unlike what we typically think of as a “mushroom,” Ascomycetes don’t form caps and stems but produce structures called fruiting bodies, which release spores to reproduce. And growing up to 20 inches and weighing nearly 5 pounds, D. rex’s are huge.

Within the Ascomycetes, the giant fungus constitutes an entirely new biological family (the broader classification of genus and species), meaning that it has no close relatives—at least that we know of. While 6,000 fungal species have been documented in Yunnan, mycologists believe the province known for its biological diversity could easily harbor 100,000

“The discovery illustrates how much fungus biodiversity remains to be discovered in East Asian forests,” Indunil C. Senanayake, an associate professor at the Kunming Institute of Botany and the lead author of the paper, said in a press release.

Senanayake and his research team conducted surveys in the Nanban tropical rainforest, where they came across large, whitish fruiting bodies growing in the soil near the roots of dying trees. They documented and collected fungi growing in a wide variety of shapes like cylinders, spindles, and cupped palms. The fruiting bodies appeared to develop finger-like branches upon maturity. They found that the above-ground portions are covered with white, powdery fibers that turn dark yellow as the fungi mature, while the portions buried in the soil are black and smooth.

The fruiting bodies dried rapidly when the scientists took samples back to the lab. They found that the interiors were dense and sponge-like, appearing white or yellow with no strong odor or taste. (Don’t try this experiment at home.)

Analyzing the DNA of these fungi, the researchers reconstructed a “molecular clock,” which revealed that D. rex diverged from other species approximately 65 million years ago. This is the end of the Cretaceous period, immediately following the mass extinction that is believed to have wiped out three-quarters of all plant and animal species, including the dinosaurs. That means D. rex and T. rex never met, but this era was likely a boon for fungi. Large quantities of dead plant material became available following the die-off, which could have provided new ecological opportunities for fungi and other organisms that decompose such matter.

Genomic analyses, however, indicated that D. rex doesn’t have nearly as many decomposition-related genes as the scientists expected. Compared to some of its closest relatives that also feast on plant matter, the huge fungus has only about half the usual number of decomposition-related genes.

Senanayake has a theory about this. Although they have so far only found the fruiting bodies around dying plants, the fungus may be leading a “double life”—living inside healthy trees and then feeding on dead plant material when the hosts die. That could perhaps explain how D. rex is able to store enough energy to build the massive fruiting bodies.

This is just a theory for now, but the giant fungi undoubtedly do need a surplus of energy to grow so large. Senanayake hypothesizes that the benefit of these large fruiting bodies is that they tower higher than the surrounding plants and fallen leaves, which could be advantageous for wind-mediated spore dispersal in a dense forest. “However, such a large structure requires substantial energy investment, suggesting that this strategy likely evolved only where the ecological benefits outweighed the costs.”

#411. Feds arrest top official of healthcare company, alleging illegal drug importation

U.S. authorities are accusing the two top officials of a Canadian company of operating a long-running scheme to illegally import prescription drugs into the U.S., according to court records filed Tuesday.

John Thomas Howard, treasurer and a director of the company, CANARX, was arrested Sunday on smuggling and conspiracy charges, according to the records.

Bob Howard, his brother, who is president and a director of CANARX, is also wanted in the federal investigation, according to an affidavit from Dustin Dobbs, a special agent with the Food and Drug Administration's Office of Criminal Investigations. He has not yet been taken into custody.

Besides conspiracy and smuggling, the arrest warrant said John Thomas Howard was charged with introducing misbranded drugs into interstate commerce and illegal drug importation. No plea has been filed.

CANARX was featured in a CNBC investigation last year that revealed how the company and others known as alternative funding programs, or AFPs, are becoming more pervasive around the U.S. as drug costs skyrocket. The investigation found these companies have penetrated the country's healthcare system by offering high-cost specialty drugs at more affordable prices through private and public employers. In some cases, employers are requiring their staff to use an AFP.

AFPs often obtain the medications overseas and ship them directly to patients, a practice that federal authorities have said is illegal and poses safety risks. CNBC traced the trail of drugs from abroad to the U.S. via a supply chain that the pharmaceutical companies producing the medications have not authorized.

On its website, CANARX, founded in 2002 and based in Windsor, Ontario, Canada, described its business as the first international prescription provider for the U.S. and the safest such program in the industry. The company said it sources its medications from Canada, the United Kingdom, Australia and New Zealand.

But the affidavit said that the FDA's investigation found "there is probable cause to believe that (CANARX) illegally imported or caused the illegal importation of prescription drugs into the United States …."

The affidavit does not specify which medications the agency believes were illegally imported. The criminal complaint covers the period from Feb. 22, 2017, through April 15, 2026.

The company's general counsel, Joseph Morris, told CNBC in a statement: "The Howards deny all the charges against them, will defend vigorously, and expect to be vindicated in court."

The company has been under FDA scrutiny for several years. In 2023, the agency seized medication being shipped by CANARX that it says was potentially misbranded and counterfeit, according to an email sent to a patient that was obtained by CNBC.

And in 2019, the FDA sent a warning letter to the company, saying it was operating "in a manner that substitutes the FDA-approved drugs prescribed by the U.S. healthcare provider with unapproved drugs." Morris, in response to that warning, argued CANARX was not misleading patients, but agreed for the company to stop importing drugs that need to be administered by a doctor.

In an interview as part of the CNBC investigation, Morris said last year that the medicines shipped to U.S. patients come from highly regulated countries. He said CANARX was operating legally.

The central question for AFPs is the legality of importing prescription drugs into the U.S. for personal use. The FDA allows Americans to import medications for individual use in some cases if the drug is not available domestically. That is the basis on which the AFPs built their models for shipping medicines directly to patients.

But an FDA spokesperson told CNBC last year that "in most circumstances, a person cannot import prescription drugs from other countries into the U.S. ... as substitutes for FDA-approved drugs."

In a separate civil lawsuit that is pending, Gilead Sciences accused CANARX and other companies of illegally importing Biktarvy, its medication to treat HIV.

In a statement to CNBC on Wednesday, a Gilead spokesperson said the company "has taken strong legal action to protect patients and the integrity of the U.S. medicines supply chain. We welcome the government's efforts to hold accountable those whose conduct may put patients at risk."

CANARX has not responded to the Gilead lawsuit.

— CNBC's Paige Tortorelli and Melissa Lee contributed to this report

#410. It’s Getting Too Dry for Even Cactuses to Survive

The humble cactus is an icon of resiliency. Stuck in a desert with little rain and abundant searing sun, these plants don’t just survive, they thrive—and in turn provide habitat for owls and other birds, and flowers for bats and other pollinators.

Yet cacti are in serious trouble, due to habitat loss and the poaching of species for private collections. Now we can add worsening droughts to those woes, according to a new study in the journal Nature. Researchers modeled a future of not just relentlessly rising temperatures but also the attendant dry spells, finding that the majority of nearly 1,000 cactus species will lose habitat as the climate radically changes. More than a quarter of those species could lose at least 80 percent of that habitat by 2100.

“The public perception of cacti tends to be of these organisms that are extremely drought-adapted and are just resilient, can handle anything that you throw at them,” said Michiel Pillet, a conservation ecologist at the University of South Florida and lead author of the paper. “In reality, cacti are absolutely amazing—and they’re my favorite plant group, clearly—but they’re not that different from other plants.”

That means cacti need water. In the deserts of the southwestern United States, for example, they rely on seasonal monsoon rains, which tend to make July or August the wettest month in a given year. Because this is still a desert, cacti have evolved a range of adaptations to survive, like a waxy skin that helps them retain that bounty of rainwater. “Just like other plants, they evolved under conditions that they’re used to,” Pillet said. “When you take them outside of those conditions, they might not be able to handle that.”

Think of these cacti like sick hospital patients. Someone might be admitted for heat-related illness, as their body struggles to cool itself. At the same time, though, they might be dehydrated. Cacti are dealing with the same stresses, as global temperatures rise and dry spells intensify. “When we consider drought conditions, then it turns out that they’re much, much sicker than we previously thought,” Pillet said. “These results really do change the outlook of cacti, and most likely plants more broadly under climate change.”

Instead of simply modeling these threats going forward, the researchers turned to the citizen science platform iNaturalist, where people upload photos of plants and animals. This provided 650,000 observations of cacti in the wild, helping paint a clearer picture of how species might be faring.

Really, this isn’t a simple matter of cacti withering and dying. Sure, if relentless droughts take hold, individuals can’t get water. But the stress could be temporally complex, too. “Now we can move towards questions of: What life stages are most affected?” Pillet said. “Is it the adults or is it the seedlings? But also, which processes? Is it reduced growth or is it increased mortality? Or are plants failing to flower?”

Put another way: Cactus species could be suffering from sublethal effects of drought—the lack of water may not kill them immediately but still interfere with their reproduction. And without procreation, populations shrink. In the Sonoran Desert, for example, the iconic, towering saguaro is reproducing less frequently during prolonged droughts, because they’re not always flowering.

Part of the problem is that while desert ecosystems may look tough and hardened overall, they’re in fact quite fragile. “They don’t recover quickly once they are disturbed, or once they are degraded, just because there’s so little water and such higher temperatures,” said Marcos Robles, the Nature Conservancy’s lead scientist in Arizona, who wasn’t involved in the research. “It takes multiple decades to recover, and in some cases, they don’t recover.”

Now drought is compounding with other stressors, like habitat loss and poaching, to drive cactus species to the brink. On the opposite end of the spectrum, too much water from rising seas has led to the local extinction of the Key Largo tree cactus in Florida. Higher nighttime temperatures are a problem, too, since that’s when saguaro and other cacti respire to avoid water loss. “If the nighttime temperatures are very high, they can’t do it,” said Rod Haenni, president of the nonprofit Cactus and Succulent Society of America, who wasn’t involved in the paper. “Then if you water on top of that, the plants just rot.”

Still, some cactus species may persist by picking up and leaving. Well, not individual plants, but populations could creep into areas with climates that more closely match what the species adapted to all those millennia ago. A similar thing is happening in the Arctic, actually, where some plants are marching farther north in “search” of increasingly agreeable weather and more water.

In the end, the purpose of this sort of research is to better inform where conservation professionals might concentrate their efforts. They can’t stop droughts, of course, but they can evacuate enough cacti to establish populations in botanical gardens, where they can be cared for as the climate grows more unrecognizable to even the hardiest desert vegetation. Species can even be reintroduced to the wild in some cases, so long as drought and high temperatures don’t remain a threat to a population.

Governments, too, need to protect the lands where cacti are threatened, Haenni said. This new research, he added, could make that happen. “Hopefully, it’ll wake people up,” Haenni said. “But the problem is that a lot of the governments involved are indifferent to this. There are a lot of people in the countries who are not indifferent, but they’re not running the governments.”

This story originally appeared on Grist and is part of the Climate Desk collaboration.

#405. Scout Motors has 212,000 vehicle reservations as $2 billion South Carolina plant nears completion

BLYTHEWOOD, S.C. — Electric vehicle startup Scout Motors has notched roughly 212,000 reservations for its upcoming EVs as the company nears completion of its new $2 billion plant here, CEO Scott Keogh told CNBC.

The reservations are for the Volkswagen-backed company's Terra pickup truck and Traveler SUV, which are expected to start full production next year before deliveries begin to customers in 2028.

Reservations do not necessarily translate to sales, as the U.S. automotive industry has learned during the past decade, especially with highly anticipated all-electric trucks such as the Ford F-150 Lightning, Tesla Cybertruck and others that garnered strong interest that didn't lead to meaningful numbers of purchases.

Keogh said he expects Scout could roughly double those reservations by the time customer production begins, but he said the more important thing is to convert early interest into actual sales as production nears.

"It's been a consistent drumbeat," Keogh said during an interview at Scout's plant site outside Columbia. "I think we could easily be up over 400,000 with reservations without doing too much effort. The big thing for us is how much of the initial production can we 100% presell."

Keogh said achieving an order book of 12 to 18 months "would be great" and is something he thinks the company can do before production. Scout on Thursday said plant construction is "in its final phase."

Scout's vehicles will be offered as all-electric versions as well as electric hybrid variants, known as extended-range electric vehicles, or EREVs, that include an internal combustion engine as a backup generator for the electric motors and batteries.

Scout, a former American vehicle brand from 1961 to 1980, was expected to exclusively offer EVs in a bid for the German automaker to expand its presence in the U.S. However, slower-than-expected adoption of EVs and higher costs led the company to decide to offer EREVs as well.

Keogh said extended-range and SUV models are dominating reservations thus far, with roughly 87% choosing EREV models and 75% being SUVs.

The CEO also said the company is drawing interest from its target markets, which include owners of off-road vehicles such as the Ford Bronco and Jeep Wrangler as well as full-size pickup trucks and U.S. EV leader Tesla.

Given the interest signaled by reservations, Keogh said the company is expected to launch with the EREVs first, followed by the EVs. He also said the company plans to launch with mid-level models first, a deviation from many companies that begin with their highest-priced models.

Both the Traveler and Terra are expected to start at under $60,000. The EREV models are expected to feature more than 500 miles of range, according to the company, compared with 300 miles of range for the all-electric models.

"The No.1 thing for me is I want to go get the best volume as soon as possible," Keogh said. "In my mind, it's how quick can you get to 100,000 units? It's at 100,000 units where you can start to punch through."

His comments come months after Rivian Automotive received criticism online for launching its R2 SUV with its highest-priced models first, despite that being a common practice across the automotive industry.

Keogh said Scout's South Carolina plant is about 85% complete, but supporting infrastructure and a $300 million supplier park continue to be installed.

Reservations for Scout's vehicles initially opened in October 2024, when it revealed the Traveler and Terra, which feature sleek, yet rugged, exteriors that pay some homage to the brand's historic models.
